1181. JOHN JOHNSON was indicted for stealing a handkerchief, value 4 d., the goods of Frederick Monks, from his person: to which he pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18450512-1182]
1182. ISAAC PARKER was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 6 d., the goods of Charles James Norris, from his person.
CHARLES JAMES NORRIS . I am clerk to Mr. Bell, of Chancery-lane. On Monday, at a quarter to seven o'clock, I was at Greenwich Fair(I was touched on the shoulder, and asked if I had lost my handkerchief—I felt and missed it—this produced is it—I had used it not two minutes before.
JOHN WHITE ( policeman.) I was at the fair, and saw the prisoner go behind the prosecutor, take the handkerchief from his pocket, and walk away—I went after him, and caught hold of him, as he was in the act of taking it out of his own pocket.
GEORGE BROWN ( policeman.) I was with "White, and saw the prisoner take the handkerchief out of the prosecutor's pocket
Prisoner's Defence. I was going out of the fair, as there was a bustle; I put my hand in my pocket, and felt something that was not my own, and while taking it out the policeman took me.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18450512-1183]
1183. WILLIAM JOHNSON was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief value 6 d., the goods of Hannah Rogers, from her person.
HANNAH ROGERS . I live in Somerset-place, Rotherhithe. On the 12th of May I was at Greenwich-fair, and lost my handkerchief from my pocket—it was safe about a quarter of an hour before—this now produced is it.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 180.) I was at the fair, and saw the prisoner put his hand into the prosecutrix's pocket, and take from it this hand-kerchief—I caught him by the arm and he dropped it—I picked it up.
Prisoner's Defence. A boy standing before me took the handkerchief and threw it down by the side of me.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Four Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18450512-1184]
1184. THOMAS HAYS was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of May, 1 cigar-case, value 1 s.; 2 cheroots, 4 d.; and 3 cigars, 6 d., the goods of Charles Watson, from his person.
CHARLES WATSON . I am a sergeant of the City-police, and live in Green Arbour-court, Moorfields. On the 14th of May I was in Green wick fair, in plain clothes—I heard a scuffle behind me, and on turning round observed my cigar-case in the bands of a man in plain clothes, who afterwards turned out to be an officer.
JAMES RAPSEY ( police-constable R 102.) I was at Greenwich on the 14th of May, about four o'clock—I saw the prisoner and another go towards Watson, lift the tail of his coat, draw the cigar-case from bis pocket, and pass it to the other, who I immediately laid hold oft and took it from him—Watson turned round, and seeing the case in my hand, rescued the prisoner, and took the cigar-case from me, thinking I bad taken it.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 180.) I did not see the prisoner take the case—I saw him lift the prosecutor's coat tail, and took him into custody.
Prisoner's Defence. I was looking at Richardson's show; there was a crush of people, and I saw a boy taking the cigar-case out of the gentleman's pocket; the officers caught bold of him and took it from him; lie ran away, and the officer got bold of me.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Six Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18450512-1185]
1185. MARY ANN SEXTON was indicted for stealing 1 boa, value 10 s.; 1 necklace, 5 s.; 6 towels, 3 s.; 1 pillow-case, 2 s.; 3 shirts, 2 s.; 2 napkins, 1 s.; 1 pair of socks, 6 d. 3 petticoats, 6 d.; 1 cap, 6 d.; 4 yards of ribbon, 6 d.; 1 yard of printed cotton, 6 d.; 2 printed books, 1 s.; 1 salt cellar, 6 d.; 2 wine glasses, 2 s.; 1 basin, 6 d.; and 1 collar, 2 s., the goods of Barber Tuck, her master.
BARBER TUCK . I am a grocer, and live in Tranquil Vale, Blackheath. The prisoner came into my service on the 30th of January, and on the 30th of April she had leave to go out for a holiday—my wife opened her box, which was in her bed-room, with a key we had about the house, and some things of mine were in it—the constable was afterwards sent for—the box was opened in the passage in the prisoner's presence, and in it was
found some duplicates, some towels, a necklace, some children's clothes, and many other articles—the articles produced are mine, and have my mark on them.
JOHN TIGHE . I am a pawnbroker and reside at Deptford Broadway. I produce some trowsers and a frock, which were pawned on the 17th of Feb., for 2 s., but I cannot say by whom—this duplicate was given to the person who pawned them—here is a counterpane and some other articles pawned by the prisoner in the name of Heathfield—I have known her some time—I took them in of her.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-constable R 84.) I went to Mr. Tuck about nine o'clock at night, on the 30th of April—I waited till the prisoner came home, and Mr. Tuck charged her on suspicion of robbing him—she said, "Me rob him—what of?"—I asked her to give me the key of her box, she took it from her pocket and gave it to me—the box was searched in her presence, and a great number of things were found in it—amongst the rest these six towels, this necklace, these duplicates, and other things—I afterwards went to the house of a person at Lewisham, where a child of the prisoner's is, and there I found this wine-glass, basin, and egg-cup, and a handkerchief which Mr. Tuck identifies—after the prisoner had been searched at the station-house she said, "I am very sorry for what I have done; I hope my master will not come against me."
SARAH TUCK . I am the wife of Barber Tuck. I know these children's clothes—I opened the prisoner's box with a common key of another trunk—almost any trunk key would open it.
Prisoner's Defence. My box was unlocked with a false key; I never put the things in it.
GUILTY . Aged 27.
[trialAccount t18450512-1186]
1186. MARY ANN SEXTON was again indicted for stealing 2 towels, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 pillow-case, 1 s.; 2 night-gowns,2 s.; 1 shift, 1 s.; 2 pairs of stockings, 2 s.; 1 necklace, 2 s.; 2 ornaments, 4 s.; 2 pieces of mineral, 6 d.; 3 shells, 6 d.; I basket, 3 s.; and 1 wine-glass, 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Francis Armstrong Nugent, her master.
SUSANNAH NUGENT . I am the wife of Francis Armstrong Nugent. The prisoner was in my service about two months—she left about the 4th of Jan.—these towels, pillow-case, night-gowns, and other things are ours, I had them in use when the prisoner was in my service—I missed them, but I did not know who had got them.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-constable R 84.) I searched tne prisoner's box in her presence at Mr. Tuck's—I found in it one towel, the pillow-case, night-gown, and some other things which Mrs. Nugent identifies—at the place where the prisoner's child was, I found this other towel and a wine-glass of Mrs. Nugent's—I asked the prisoner whose the things were, she made no answer—I asked if she had lived with a person at Greenwich, she said, "Yes, with Mr. Nugent."
Prisoner. They were all put into my box; I never put them there.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years.
JOHN CARPENTER stated that other property was found in the prisoner's box belonging to four other persons with whom she had lived, and he had two certificates of character by which the prisoner obtained situations, but which turned out to be false.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

[trialAccount t18450512-1189]
1189. MADELINE STEWART was indicted for stealing 1 handlerchief, value 2 s. the goods of Thomas Rix.
JOHN WILSON PARKER . I am apprentice to Thomas Rix, of Woolwich. On the 3rd of May, the prisoner came to the shop for some blond—she took a handkerchief off the counter, put it under her arm, and walked out with it—I fetched her back with the handkerchief under her arm—she said she had asked me about the handkerchief, and then she said she had paid for it, but she had not,
Prisoner. Did I not buy some blond and some ribbon, and give you a sovereign: and why did you give me only 18 s. if I had not bought the handkerchief? the policeman only took 18 s. from me.
Witness. She bought three yards of blond at 1 d. a yard, and one yard of ribbon at 2 d.—she gave me a sovereign—I gave her 19 s. 6 d. and 1 d.—I gave her full change all but 5 d.—I cannot remember what coin I gave her.
GEORGE KELLY ( police-constable R 167.) I took the prisoner—she had a half-sovereign and 18 s. in her hand—she bad no sixpence and no penny.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18450512-1190]
1190. WILLIAM JENKINS and HENRY JOSEPH FINCH were indicted for stealing from the person of John Mark 1 purse, value 6 d.; 12 sovereigns, 6 half-sovereigns, and 1 10 l. bank-note, his property; and that Finch had been before convicted of felony.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN MARK . I am a gunner in the Royal Artillery, at Woolwich. On the 28th of April I had been to London—I had a 30 l. bank-note, which I changed at Mr. Crump's, in Goswell-road—I received for it a 10 l. banknote and sovereigns, half-sovereigns, and silver—I put the note and money in my purse into my watch-pocket—I did not take the number of the note, but I observed some writing on it which enables me to know it again—I then went to Dalston, and from there to London-bridge, to get back to Woolwich—it was then past eleven o'clock at night, I dare say—I was perfectly sober—I went to a stand of cabs and called for one—Jenkins came up as the driver, and brought the cab to the curb-stone—I asked what he would take me to Woolwich for—he said 8 s.—I said I could not afford that, and offered him 2 s.—he said he could not take me for 2 s.—Finch was standing near him, and he said, "O take the gentleman down, take him down for 2 s., he will likely stand a pot of ale on the road"—that was the first I had seen of Finch—Jenkins said very well, he would do
it—I got in, and Jenkins said, "Will you allow this man (meaning Finch) to go with you as far as Greenwich in the cab?"—Finch was by at the time—I said, "Yes, you may get in," and he got in—the cab went on between half and two-thirds of the way between London and Greenwich, and it there stopped—Finch said, "Draw up here," or "We had better stop here"—Jenkins drew up by a public-house on the left hand side—Finch got out on the right side of the cab, went round, and went into the public-house, and, as I presume, got a pot of ale—I paid for it—I did not get out, and cannot say whether Jenkins was on his box or not—Finch was away five or six minutes—he then came to the left side of the cab and said to me, "Here, take a glass of this ale"—I said, "Is it good?"—he said, "Yes, very good"—I drank it and gave him the glass, which he took into the house again, then came and took his seat in the cab —I then fell asleep, and did not awake till we got to Woolwich—I was still asleep, Jenkins came and awoke me up, and said, "You are at Woolwich"—Finch was then gone—I said, "Very well"—I got out of the cab, and found Jenkins in the act of getting on the box again, never having asked me for his money—I said, "Oh! I have not paid you, I must pay you"—I went to feel for my money, and found my purse and money were gone—I said, "Good G—! I have been robbed; you know something about it; where is the man that you asked my permission to let ride with me?"—I seized hold of Jenkins and said, "I will detain you until I give you in charge"—two policemen came up—I gave him, his horse and cab, in charge—I have seen the 10 l. note since, and identified it—this is it—here is the word "York" upon it—when we arrived at the station I gave so minute a description of Finch that he was taken the next day.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. What had you been doing that day? A. I had got leave from my commanding officer to come to town on Sunday the day before—I went to No. 21, St. James-street—the persons I went to see were not at home, and on the Monday morning I called there again—I had dined at four o'clock in the afternoon—I had a mutton-chop and a glass of porter—I took about three glasses of port wine within two hours after my dinner—I had about a couple more glasses between eight and nine o'clock, which made five glasses after my dinner—I took nothing else till I had the ale in the cab —it was, past eleven when I got into the cab.
Jenkins. Q. Was you sober, or was you not? A. I have sworn I was sober—when I came to you at London-bridge, you brought your cab to the curb—I swear I had only one glass of ale on the road, which was at the first house you stopped at—whether you stopped at any other house after I lost my senses I cannot tell.
WILLIAM CRUMP . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Goswell-street-road—the prosecutor came to my house on Monday evening, the 28th of April—I changed a 30 l. note for him—I gave him this 10 l. note, and the rest in gold and silver—here is my writing on the note, and I had previously taken the number of it, whrch I have here—the prosecutor was quite sober then—here is the name of the person I took the note from on it.
MICHAEL GARVEY ( police-constable R 87.) I was on duty on Tuesday morning, the 29th of April—I took Jenkins from the prosecutor, and took him to the station—the prosecutor accused him, and said, "Where is the man that was in the cab along with me?"—Jenkins said, "I do not know, I put him down at Greenwich, I have no knowledge of him"—this was in
Church-street, Woolwich, before we started for the station—when we got there Jenkins said he had seen the person who was in the cab before, and he should know him again if we were to see him.
THOMAS BALDWIN ( police-sergeant L 13.) I was in the Westminster-road, at half-past six o'clock on Tuesday morning, the 29th of April—I went into the Oxford Arms, and saw Finch, in company with some men, three of whom were cab men—Finch was treating them, and I saw him take some money out of his right-hand pocket—I saw him count seven sovereigns and some silver—I told him he had better take care of his money—he said it was all right, they were all his men, and he was treating them.
JAMES CUDDY ( police-sergeant L 20.) I had heard a description of Finch—I went to Batty's Theatre on Tuesday evening, the 29th of April, and found Finch in the gallery—I took him into custody—I found on him this 10 l. note, 1 l. 11 s. 2 d. in silver, and 5 d. in copper.
Jenkins's Defence. On the 28th the soldier hired my cab, he was with another party, and each of them were smoking cigars; he asked what I would take him for, I said 8 s.; he said he could not afford that, he would give me 2 s.; we hesitated some minutes, and then I agreed to take him for 2 s.; the cab was not drawn down; he said he would give me some ale on the road; we stopped at three places; he drank two or three glasses of ale; I stopped once in Tooley-street, once at Mill pond-bridge, and once further on; I got to Woolwich, and he was asleep; I said, "This is Woolwich;" he said, "Where is the man?" I said he got out at Green-wich; the policeman came up; I told him the circumstance, and he said to the prosecutor, "Will you give the prisoner in charge?" he said "Yes;" I have witnesses to prove he was intoxicated when he took my cab,
WILLIAM COCKRILL ( police-constable L 108.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner Finch's former conviction, which I got at Kingston-upon-Thames—( read—Convicted 28th Dec. 1833, and confined three months, and whipped)—the prisoner is the person.
JENKINS — GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for Ten Years.
FINCH — GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18450512-1191]
1191. PHILIP SHARPE was indicted for stealing 1 writing-desk, value 5 s.; 1 breast-pin, 2 s. 6 d.; 1 watch-key, 6 d.; 1 medal, 2 s. 6 d.; and 1 printed book, 1 s.; the goods of John Fiddes.
JOSEPH FLEMMING . I am steward on board the sloop of war Alecto, which is in Woolwich Dockyard. On the 25th of April I was ordered to remove John Fiddes's clothes and goods from that vessel to the Ship tavern—I got the prisoner and others to assist me—amongst other things there was a desk, a breast-pin, and a watch-key—I afterwards missed the desk—this is it.
NICHOLAS MOULD . I keep a public-house at Woolwich. On the 25th of April, between two and three o'clock, the prisoner brought this desk to my house, and asked me to let him put it down a few minutes.
GEORGE GURNEY ( police-constable R 303.) I took the prisoner—I found this watch-key and breast-pin upon him.
JOSEPH FLEMMING re-examined. This breast-pin belongs to John Fiddes.
Prisoner's Defence. I was standing at the Dock-gate; this gentleman came and asked me if I would have a job; he took me and some others
down into the cabin, and made us nearly drunk; the things were put into a cart; the men broke the cart, and the things all rolled about; I took the desk into the public-house, and forgot it again.
JOSEPH FLEMMING . I did not make them drunk—I gave them a small drop of rum after the job was over—the prisoner got drunk afterwards.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18450512-1192]
1192. WILLIAM GRACE was indicted for stealing 1 gelding, price 6 l., the property of William Newett, his master.
WILLIAM NEWETT . I am a carman, and live in Shooter's Hul-road—the prisoner was in my service. On Friday morning, the 4th of April, I gave him a gelding, and told him to go to the Catherine Wheel inn with it, and to see the ostler, who would direct him to where I had the gelding from—I had bought it, but did not like it, and wanted to return it—he was to take it to the man, and if he did not take it in, he was to take it back to the ostler at the Catherine Wheel—I saw the prisoner again on the Sunday morning—he said he had left the horse all right at Mr. Adams's, the man I had it of—I said, "Very well"—I had changed another horse away, and was to give 8 l. for this one, but it was not sound, and I returned it.
GEORGE HOOK . I am ostler at the Catherine Wheel in the Borough. On Friday morning, 4th of April, about ten o'clock, the prisoner came with a hone—he asked if I knew it—I said "No," hut I looked again, and said I did—he asked if I knew where Mr. Adams lived—I said yes, about 300 yards from the Catherine Wheel, the second public-house on the right hand—he said he should go with tire horse there, and return and give me an answer about it, but he did not return.
Prisoner. There was no such words; I said I was to leave the horse with a man named Hook; I said to him, "You take it in and keep it a week till my master comes;" he said he could not take it in; he directed me out of the yard; I had no name or number to go to. Witness. No, he told me he would go with the horse, and I said if he could not find the man to come back to me again.
THOMAS MOOR . I am foreman to Mr. Giddings, a horse-slaughter. The prisoner came to me that morning to sell the horse, as he, said, for the best price he could get for it—it was a glandered horse, and unfit to be about—I paid him 2 l. for it, and he gave me a receipt.
Prisoner's Defence. My master came, and gave me 3 s. which was all I received of him for three weeks; he sent me to the place with the hocse, and if I could not get rid of it there I was to sell it, and to bring the money home; these were the last words he said to me; the ostler refused taking it in, as it was glandered; he said it was more than his place was worth; my master told me I was to take it to this man, and he had better have the money he could get for it, than nothing.
WILLIAM NEWETT re-examined. Q. Did the prisoner tell you he had sold it, and give you the 2 l.? A. No—he said he left it at Mr. Adah's, and tied it up in the stable—I did not know the horse was glandered, but it was not sound, and would not do for me.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18450512-1193]
1193. SARAH ANN ADAMS was indicted for stealing 2 half-crowns the monies of William White, from his person.
WILLIAM WHITE . I am a shipwright. On the 20th of April I was at a public-house in Woolwich—the prisoner was by my side—I had 10 s., when I went in—I sat there, and had two or three pints of beer—I did not know what I did altogether, but a gentleman called me out, and asked if I had been robbed—I found I had lost two half-crowns from my right band pocket.
Prisoner. At the Duke on Horseback you gave me two half-crowns to go with you. Witness. I did not.
JOHN M'DONALD . I was at the Duke on Horseback, between ten and eleven o'clock at night, on the 20th of April—I saw the prisoner come in with the prosecutor—she asked him for 1 s., which he gave her, and no sooner bad he done that than she put her hand into his right hand pocket, and took out two half-crowns, put them to her hair, and then put then into her mouth—I went to her, and said, "Do you chew tobacco?"—she said "Yes"—I said, "You had better put that quid out of your mouth"—she went out—I followed, and gave her in charge.
JOHN WALLEN ( police-constable R 298.) I took the prisoner—she denied having any money, and all knowledge of it—I took her hand, and had a violent struggle with her—I at last got two half-crowns from her.
Prisoner's Defence. He gave me the 5 s.; M'Donald wanted me to give half a crown to spend, and I would not.
GUILTY .** Aged 28.— Transported for Ten Years.
(John Wallen stated that the prisoner had been thirty-two times in custody.)
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18450512-1194]
1194. MARGARET SKELDING was indicted for stealing 2 waist-coats, value 6 s., the goods of John Daniel Delaney.
WILLIAM LEACH . I am shopman to John Daniel Delaney, pawnbroker, High-street, Woolwich. On the 3rd of May I missed this satin waistcoat, which I had seen half an hour before hanging in the shop—I know nothing of the prisoner.
GEORGE STEVENS . I am shopman to Messrs. Davis, pawnbroker, Woolwich. The prisoner came there on the 3rd of May, between seven and eight o'clock—she asked me 5 s. on two waistcoats, and gave the name of Ann Murphy—I recollect her, as there was a gown hanging there, which she took down, and took it away with her—these are the two waist-coats she brought—her husband brought the gown back to me.
WILLIAM LEACH re-examined. These waistcoats are Mr. Delaney's.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( police-constable R 122.) I took the prisoner on Monday, the 5th of May, at her own home—I told her what she was charged with—she said it was not her, she knew nothing about it—I said, "I saw you at Mr. Delaney's shop on Saturday evening"—she said, "Yes, I was there, I went to redeem my husband's trowsers"—she gave me up ten duplicates, but they relate to her own property, I believe.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1195. WILLIAM CUMMINGS and THOMAS BROWN were indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of Peter Dow, from his person.
PETER DOW . I am a teacher, and live at Lee Park, Blackheath. On the 12th of May I was in Greenwich fair—I had my handkerchief safe—I did not miss it till I was touched on the shoulder—this is it
WILLIAM NORTON . I am a tailor, and live at Deptford. I was at Green-wich fair on the afternoon of the 12th of May—I saw the two prisoners behind the prosecutor—I saw Brown take this handkerchief out of the prosecutor's pocket, and hand it to Cummings—I informed the prosecutor of it, and held the prisoners till the officer came up—in the mean time Cummings dropped the handkerchief, and the prosecutor picked it up.
JOHN EVANS ( police-constable R 190) I saw the prisoners at the fair attempting to pick pockets on several occasions—I took them into custody—Cummings ried to escape, but I took him.
Cummings's Defence. I was looking at a show, and some boy threw a handkerchief at me.
Brown's Defence. I was standing by the side of the prosecutor; the witness took hold of me.
CUMMINGS — GUILTY . Aged 13.
BROWN — GUILTY . Aged 11.
Confined Three Months.
Before Edward Buttock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18450512-1196]
1196. JOHN EDWARDS was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of George Wintle Roberts, from his person.
GEORGE WINTLE ROBERTS . I live in Park-street, Islington. On the 14th of May I was at Greenwich fair, about half-past seven o'clock in the evening—I lost my handkerchief, which I had had safe about half a minute before—the moment I lost it the officer inquired of me if I had lost it.
JAMES RAPSEY ( police-constable R 102.) I saw the prisoner at the fair try a great many pockets—he went behind Mr. Roberts, and closed behind him—in half a minute he turned away—I spoke to Mr. Roberts, then turned and apprehended the prisoner—he had this handkerchief.
Prisoner's Defence. I saw the handkerchief under my feet; I picked it up, got on the foot-path, and the policeman collared me.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Six Months.

1197. MICHAEL MAROONEY and ANDREW MANYON were indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Charles Berridge, from his person; to which
MANYON pleaded GUILTY .* Aged 13.— Confined Six Months.
CHARLES BERRIDOE . I am a coal-merchant, and live in Vauxhall-street. On the 12th of May, about half-past seven o'clock in the evening, I was in Greenwich fair—an officer touched me on the shoulder, and I found that I had lost two handkerchiefs—the one that was found on the prisoner Manyon I had wiped my face with a few minutes before—this is the same pattern as the one I had—I do not know it by any mark—I have every reason to believe it is mine.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 180.) I was in Greenwich fair—I saw the two prisoners in company—they went and closed behind the prosecutor, and I saw Manyon lift the tail of his coat—I did not see them take the handkerchief, but I suspected them, and spoke to the prosecutor
—he said he had lost something—I went and took the prisoner—Manvon I said, "I have stolen the gentleman's handkerchief"—he put his hand into the flap of his trowsers, and took out this handkerchief—the prosecutor said, "That is not mine"—I took the prisoners to the station, and I fonud in Manyon's waistcoat this other handkerchief, which the prosecutor does identify.
HENRY JORDAN . I am a butcher. I was at the fair—I saw Manyon feel the prosecutor's coat-tail pocket—Marooney was standing by his side.
Marooney's Defence. I did not know this boy, only I thought I had seen him; I asked if he lived in such a place, and if he was going home; that was all I said to him.
MAROONEY — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18450512-1198]
1198. JOHN SULLIVAN and BRIDGET COLLINS were indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 6 d. the goods of George Stone, from his person; to which
SULLIVAN * pleaded GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.
GEORGE STONE . I live in South-street, Greenwich. On the 14th of May I was in Greenwich fair—some one spoke to me—I turned, and saw my handkerchief in Sullivan's hand—this is it—I should think I had had it safe half an hour before—I laid hold of Sullivan—the officer made a snatch at the same time, and he got the handkerchief.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-constable R 84.) On the 14th of May, I was at Greenwich—I saw the prisoners together—I watched them for half an hour—I saw Sullivan go behind several gentlemen, followed by Collins, who spread out her gown, while Sullivan felt their pockets—I then saw Sullivan go behind the prosecutor—Collins was close behind him, and spread out her gown to hide him from view—I saw Sullivan take the handkerchief—he was in the act of giving it to Collins at the time I took it from him.
Collins's Defence. I never saw it.
COLLINS — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.
SURREY CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18450512-1199]
1199. JOHN VOSPER was indicted for stealing 5 pairs of boots, value 18 s., the goods of James Berry, his master.
JAMES BERRY . I am a shoemaker, and live at Southwark. The prisoner was my journeyman for about twelve months, and lodged in the house—my workshop is on the second floor—on the 9th of April, between eleven and twelve o'clock at night, he asked me to sell him a candle, knowing I was busy, he said he wished to do as much work as possible in that week, and he would work late, or all that night—I allowed Mrs. Berry to let him have a candle—I went to rest—he went up to the work-shop—he slept in the house, and could retire when he thought proper—next morning I found he was gone, with another, who has not been taken since—five pairs of boots were also gone, part his own work, and part another workman's—I have since seen three pairs of the boots, but not the other two.
ROBERT DOUTHWAITE . I am a boot and shoemaker, and live in Tothill-street, Westminster. On the 10th of April, between eleven and twelve o'clock the prisoner came to my shop in company with another—he showed line two pairs of boots, and asked if I would purchase them—I asked where he got them—he said they were his own goods, and he was on the fuddle —I offered him 5 s. for them, which was the full value—they were not new, and had been repaired—he went away for half an hour, or three quarters of an hour, returned, and said he would take the 5 s.—I produce the boots—he promised to return in an hour or two with some more—I had not bought them above half an hour, when Mr. Berry came and identified them—next morning a man came with these for sale—Mr. Berry said they were his, and if the man came, I was to detain him—he came again—I looked outside, and saw the prisoner down below—I immediately sent for a constable and had him apprehended.
Prisoner. I sold the two pairs of boots, but the other man received the money. Witness. No, you did—I gave you two half-crowns, and you said, "We will go and have a drop of beer, as we are on the fuddle" these are the boots.
JAMES BARNICOATT ( police-constable P 149.) The prisoner and another were given into my custody—I received these two pairs of boots at the station, from the inspector on duty.
Prisoner's Defence. I pleaded guilty to that small pair as soon as I was taken into custody; I said it was my work, but I know nothing of the others; they are the other man's.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Three Months.

1201. WILLIAM ROBERTS was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Scarborough, about twelve in the night of the 18th of April, at Camber well, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, I cruet-stand, value 3 l. 10 s.; 2 cruet-tops, 8 s.; 2 knife-rests, 4 s.; 1 spoon, 5 s.; 1 neckerchief, 7 s.; 8 trays, 3 s.; 2 pence, 10 half-pence, and 12 farthings; his property.
THOMAS SCARBOROUGH . I live in Sussex-place, Kent-road, in the parish of Camberwell—I rent the house. On Friday night, the 18th of April, I went to bed at ten o'clock—my house was quite safe—the back parlour window and all the others were fastened at the top with a bolt—I saw they were all fast—next morning, between seven and eight, I found I could not get into the room—the bolt was shot inside—I had to go into the yard to get in at the window, which is nine or ten feet from the ground—I then found the window open—the spring was broken—I found all my silver gone off the sideboard—it was safe at ten o'clock the night before—the cruet-stand produced is mine, and was left in that room that night—here are four table-spoons, one gravy-spoon, a mustard-spoon, and the tops of the cruets—they all belong to me—the glasses are gone—a box, in which my wife
kept her accounts and money, was broken open, and thrown into a ditch at the back of the house—I lost property worth 10 l. altogether.
DANIEL HALEY ( policeman.) On the morning of the 19th of April I was on duty in the Kent-road, about a mile and a quarter from the prosecutor's, and about one o'clock I saw the prisoner, and stopped him, as my brother constable, who was on the other side, challenged him, and said, " Jessey, what do you do out at this time?"—he said, "I have been at a raffle for a handkerchief, and won it"—I said, "What have you in your pocket?"—he said, "Some keys"—the constable said, "Pull them out," which he did—I said, "What have you got in your trowsers?"—he said, "Nothing"—I undid his flap, and found this cruet-stand in his trowsers, and these cruet-tops in his right-hand pocket—these keys, knife-rests, handkerchief, and money, were found on him—he said he found the property in a garden in the Old Kent-road.
Prisoner. Q. Did you ever know me charged before? A. You have been before a Magistrate several times, but nothing was brought clear against you.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to a raffle; I left about half-past tweln o'clock, and in the New Kent-road I saw the things in the garden; I reached over, and put them into my pocket; there was another chap witl me; he said, "We will take them home."
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Transported for Ten Years. Before Mr. Justice Coltman.
[trialAccount t18450512-1202]
1202. THOMAS BLUNDELL, alias Smith , JEMIMA WILSON, alias Smith , and ANN WILSON , were indicted for feloniously, knowingly, and without lawful excuse, having in their custody and possession two moulds, intended for counterfeiting penny pieces.
MESSRS. SCRIVEN and WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-constable L 83.) On Sunday, the 20th of April, between one and two o'clock, I saw the prisoners Blundell and Jemima Wilson at the station-house, and, while there, I saw Blundell put something into his mouth, which he appeared to swallow with great difficulty—I said they should be searched—the female prisoner was taken to the back of the station-house by the female searcher, who afterwards returned, and said Jemima Wilson had two keys which she refused to give up—I said they would be taken from her if she did not—she then produced them—I went to No. 10, Eaton-street, New-cut, Black-friars-road, with Romain, a constable, and there found the prisoner Ann lying on the floor between the bedstead and cupboard—as we went into the room Romain turned his light on—she looked up, and said, "I thought it was my sister come," and asked where she was—I said, "She is locked up at the station, you had better get up and dress yourself"—Romain took a candle off the bedstead, and lighted it—I saw something white in the cupboard, and found a mould there, which I produce—I found a file on the same shelf, five counterfeit penny pieces, and this bag, with "Robson, plaster-of-Paris manufacturer, Mount-pleasant," on it—I found some pieces of metal in the cupboard called gets, which fit the mould, and some pieces of run metal among the cinders under the fire-place—I found some more pieces of plaster-of-Paris between the stove and the wall—there were small splashes of white metal in the front of the stove, and about the bars—I said to Ann Wilson, "I have found a mould and several counterfeit coins here, you must go to the station"—she said,
"I do not live here, it is my sister's room; I live at No. 12, and will show you"—I tried the keys to the door, and found both of them locked and unlocked the room door—it was not fastened when I went in—I asked Ann how she got in—she said she burst the door open to lie down, as her sister was out—I went with her to No. 12—she pointed out the back, parlour on the ground floor as her room—as we went in she said something I did not understand, but she seemed surprised that there was no furniture there—the room was empty—I examined the fire-place, and behind it and under it, in the ashes, I found these pieces of plaster-of-Paris, which appear like broken moulds—the floor before the hearth was loose—I took up four boards, went under the flooring, and found this broken pipkin, which appears to have burst on the fire, as the metal appears to have flown from it—there are portions of metal in it—I found other pieces of plaster-of-Paris under the floor—as I was tying them up, Ann said, "I know nothing of them"—I found one key locked and unlocked the door of No. 12, but not the other.
WILLIAM ROMAIN ( policeman.) I accompanied the witness on the 20th of April, shortly after one o'clock in the morning—the door was shut—we shoved against it, and it went open—we went up to the first floor back room—the door was shut but not fastened—it had been burst open—Ann Wilson said she did not live there, she had burst the door open to get in to sleep—I found on the mantle-piece this mould, some blue-stone, and in the cupboard a saucer, with some lead-pipe, and a piece of broken mould—this shovel was on the coals at the bottom of the cupboard, with this metal in it—here are marks at the back as if it had been on the fire—we went to No. 12 with Ann Wilson—she said that was the room she rented, but she had moved her things out the previous day—next day I accompanied the prisoners to the Magistrate—Ann said on the road, all the trouble she had got into, Blundell and her sister had brought her into—this was not in the other prisoners' hearing.
HONORA HANCOCK . I am the wife of Thomas Hancock, of No. 10, Eaton-street—my huband rents Nos. 10 and 12—the female prisoners came to me nine weeks before they were taken—Ann took the room, No. 12, and the other No. 10, where I live—Blundell was not with them then—Jemima took the room at No. 10 for herself and husband, who she said was a baker's man—I gave her the key, and in three days after he came to live there—I saw him up and down, coming to and fro, as a man would do at his own place—I saw Ann Wilson at No. 10, coming in and out to the other prisoners' room—she occupied my room at No. 12 for nine weeks before she was apprehended—they were not both taken at the same time—Jemima took No. 10 four weeks after Ann took No. 12—they both came together to take No. 12, and about four weeks after Jemima took No. 10—Ann paid the rent of 12, and Jemima of No. 10, but the last time the man paid 9 d. which, he said, was on account of rent—I gave the key of No. 12 to Ann, and that of No. 10 to Jemima—I thought Ann slept at No. 12, but do not know whether there was a bed there.
Blundell Q. When did I pay you the last money? A. The very day you was taken, about four o'clock on Saturday evening—I did not see you go out at the time, and cannot recollect whether you had your things on to go out.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin to the Mint—this plaster-of-Paris mould, found at No. 10, is intended, and has been used, to cast counterfeit penny pieces, and has impressions of both sides of a penny
piece—here are five counterfeit penny pieces which have been cast in it three of them have the gets attached to them—I have applied them to the mould and they fit it—they would have to be colpured by means of a solution of sulphate of copper before they are circulated—a pair of scissors is generally used to remove the gets, and, afterwards, a file to take off the surplus metal—here is a file with white metal in the teeth of it, and some pieces of white metal similar to the counterfeit coin, several of them in the forms of gets —here is some plaster-of-Paris which appears to have been a mould, but has no impression on it now—here is a mould found at No. 12 with the impression of both sides of a farthing on it—here is some white metal, lead and pewter, similar to the metal in the shovel—I believe the coins to be Britannia metal and pewter—I cannot tell exactly—the metal in the shovel appears of a similar description, and to have been fused in this shovel—here is a pipkin which appears to have been used to fuse in white metal, and some broken pieces of plaster without any impressions upon them—they appear to be parts of moulds.
( Blundell put in a written defence stating, that he left his room on the morning in question and met a man named Willis and his wife, who asked permission to stop in his room till the evening, that he gave them the key, that ht staid out late, and never returned home, being taken into custody for an assault, and he had no knowledge of the things being there.)
BLUNDELL — GUILTY . Aged 22.
JEMIMA WILSON — GUILTY . Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
ANN WILSON — NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

1205. WILLIAM WEBB was indicted for feloniously forging, on the 26th of March, an acquittance and receipt for 19 s. 10 d. with intent to defraud Joseph Rule: 2nd COUNT, for uttering the same with the like intent.
JOSEPH RULE . I am a grocer and cheesemonger, and live at No. 74, Suffolk-street, Southwark. On the 26th of March last, about half-past
eight o'clock in the morning, a woman came to my shop and asked for some tobacco—about two hours after she was gone, the prisoner came in—he asked me if I was a buyer of soap and candles—I said, "No"—I asked him to show me a list, which he produced—I asked if he was a traveller for that party, which was James Hibbald, whose name appeared on the list—I knew that house—the prisoner represented that he came from James Hibbald—he said so—he mentioned the name of James Hibbald, of No. 119, Bishopsgate-street—I gave him an order for a pound of rotten stone, and a pound of black lead, as a sample, to be supplied from James Hibbald's bouse—he brought the goods himself about three o'clock in the afternoon, with this paper marked A—the writing was all there with the exception of the receipt, which he wrote before me, "Settled same time, J. H."—I paid the money, 6 s., and he wrote that as the receipt—he put the "J. H." in my presence—those goods were genuine—he left a sack, and said he would call again at seven o'clock in the afternoon—about an hour after he left, the same female who had been before came in again—the prisoner called again at seven o'clock—I asked him if he could bring me 141bs. of each, the same as the sample—he said he would—he brought 14lbs. of each—I did not undo those goods till I went to Worship-street—I then undid one parcel which he had represented as 14lbs. of black lead, and there was only 4lbs. of black lead, the rest was coal—that bill came to 19 s. 10 d.—I paid him, and he signed that bill before me the same as the other—he affected to have authority to sign Hibbald's name—I was surprised at his signing "J. H."—I asked him how it was he brought me a bill, not a printed bill head—he said he was so late that night, he merely wrote it out in a hurry—when he went away, I asked him to leave me one of those lists—he refused, saying he had only one—he said he would send me one with the goods—as fast as I bought these goods a woman came to buy them again—she said she would pay me directly, but I did not let her have them—I have never seen her since—I have the goods and the bills here—I went to Mr. Hibbald's next morning, on discovering rubbish instead of the things I had ordered—he represented himself as a seller of soap and candles, and produced this list, which I looked over—I asked the price of the black lead and rotten stone—I asked if I could have 1lb. of each as a sample—he said decidedly so.
Q. But in this particular transaction, he affected to come from Hibbald, to supply you with Hibbald's goods, and gave you a receipt as having authority to do so from Hibbald? A. Yes, and by that means he got the 6 s. of my money—this is the bill and receipt—( read)—"London, March 26th, 1845: Mr. Rule, bought of J. Hibbald, 1/4 ream of glass paper, at 15 s. the ream, 3 s. 9 d.; 1lb. of stone blue, 10 d.; lib. of lead, 10 d.; lib. of rotten stone, 7 d.; total, 6 s."—that was the state in which he brought it, as a bill coming from Hibbald's, for goods with which he had supplied me in the name of Hibbald, and when I gave him the money he gave me this as Hibbald's receipt for what I had had, "Settled same time, J. H."
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you mean that was written in your presence? A. It was—not the whole of it, but "Settled same time, J. H."—the goods first supplied were genuine—the rotten stone had been damped, to weigh heavier—they were not worth the money I gave for them.
Q. Then how came you to take them? A. Well, I never did business in that way before, but having a customer coming and asking for these things, I told her I could get them for her—I saw the samples before I paid for them.
JAMES HIBBALD . I am an oilman, and live at No. 119, Bishopsgate-street. I do not know the prisoner—he was not my traveller, or in my employ—this bill was not written by my authority—my bills are printed—the heading is not exactly our form—we have the number, and name of the street—it is merely "London" here, and my bill heads are made out "James Hibbald," in full length—I sell such things as are mentioned in this paper—neither the bill, nor what purports to be the acquittance, was written by any authority from me.
COURT to JOSEPH RULE. Q. Did your faith in the genuineness of this receipt lead to a further transaction by which you were a sufferer? A. It did, to the supply of unreal goods—I believed the receipt to be the genuine authority of Mr. Hibbald, whose initials it bore, to be the receipt of his servant, in discharge of a supposed debt contracted with him—believing the genuineness of that receipt, I was led to deal with him thence-forth as the real servant of Mr. Hibbald—when I was an apprentice, I always signed a receipt "Settled same time," and my master's name, the same as this—I believed it was genuine—I gave faith to it as genuine, and acted on the footing of its being genuine.
GUILTY. Aged 30.— Judgment Respited.
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner,)
( The Jury stated their belief that the name of Hibbald was assumed without his authority, for the purpose of fraud, and was the means of putting off goods to the prosecutor as those of Hibbald, when they were not so.)
