[trialAccount t18450512-1207]
1207. WILLIAM WEBB was again indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering a forged request for the delivery of 1 gallon of gin, and 1/2 a gallon of brandy, with intent to defraud William Knott.
WILLIAM KNOTT . I keep the Stamford Arms, in Stamford-street, Blackfriars-joad. On the 4th of April the prisoner came to my house and produced this paper—I knew Mr. Godfrey, of No. 86, Bankside—I deal with him—the prisoner said he was sent by Mr. Godfrey for a gallon of gin, and half a gallon of brandy—he had a leather apron on, such as Mr. Godfrey's men wear—I supposed him his servant, and I delivered to him a gallon of gin and half a gallon of brandy, which he took away—I have known the prisoner for a long time, which removed my suspicion.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You never saw him in Mr. Godfrey's employment? A. I have seen him with Mr. Godfrey's carman—I do not know his name—I cannot say whether the prisoner said he came from Mr. Godfrey or Mr. Godfrey's.
NATHANIEL GODFREY . I am a vinegar-merchant, and live at No. 86, Bankside, Southwark. The prisoner was never in my service—this order is not my handwriting—I did not authorise any body to put my name to it, nor did I order the articles—the "Godfrey" is a very good imitation of my writing, but not the "N."
Cross-examined. Q. You have no knowledge of the prisoner? A. Not at all—a man named Thomas Turner was in my employ as carman—I
should be very glad to know where he is now—it is not like his writing—he left me on Easter Monday, long before this occurred.
THOMAS TURVEY ( policeman.) I received charge of the prisoner at Mr. Hick's, in Pearl-row, on the 4th of April—I have the goods that were obtained from Mr. Knott—they were left at a house in Gravel-lane, three or four hundred yards from Mr. Knott's—I took the person who had them to the police-office, and they would not identify the prisoner as the man who had brought them there—this was the day they were obtained—they were afterwards sent to Mr. Godfrey—( the order was here read.)
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 41.—
[trialAccount t18450512-1208]
1208. WILLIAM WEBB was again indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering, on the 4th of April, a forged request for the delivery of I gallon of gin, and I gallon of rum, with intent to defraud James Hicks.
JAMES HICKS . I live in Pearl-row, Blackfriars-road, and am a licensed victualler. On the 4th of April, about twelve o'clock, the prisoner brought this paper to me—I opened and read it—I said, "Is this Mr. Godfrey's handwriting?"—he said, "Yes"—I told him it would take me some little time to execute the order—he asked me if I had got no bottle—I said yes I had plenty of bottles, but I liked to do business so that I might be led into no dilemma or error, and I should send for a permit—he said, "How long will it be before I shall call again?"—I said it might be an hour or more, as permit writers sometimes were busy, and could not attend—he said, "Then I will call again in about an hour and a half, or two hours, and bring Mr. Godfrey's cart with me"—as soon as he went I despatched an old gentleman, who I send on errands, down to Mr. Godfrey—I told him I had some suspicion before he went—he came back in about half an hour from Mr. Godfrey's, and Mr. Godfrey's servant with him—when the prisoner came again I said to him, "This is a forgery, Mr. Godfrey did not write it, or his clerk; how came you by it?"—he said, "Mr. Godfrey's servant gave it to me"—I said, "You have got to prove that, to find Mr. Godfrey's servant"—I gave him in charge—Mr. Godfrey's servant said he knew nothing of him—I live about a mile from Bankside.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. This servant who came from Mr. Godfrey's was a livery servant, was he not? A. Yes, not a carman—the order was sealed up—I asked the prisoner if it was Mr. Godfrey's order, and he said, "Yes, I had it from Mr. Godfrey's man"—he brought no cart with him—I do not know Turner—he had no score at my house.
COURT. Q. Did the prisoner bring anything that he could carry the things away in? A. No—another of Mr. Godfrey's carmen came afterwards.
NATHANIEL GODFREY . This note is not my writing, nor written by my authority—Turner left me on the 24th of March—this is not Turner's handwriting I am certain—I never saw the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. How long was Turner in your employ? A. Two years—the servant that went to Mr. Hicks is a brother of his—he is here—one of my clerks sent him to Mr. Hicks—Turner had an opportunity of seeing me write—he could write himself, but not at all in this style—I saw his writing constantly for two years—I do not know where he is—he is no relation of mine or my wife's—I have given directions to have him apprehended—I went to Mr. Traill three times—he said he would have him published in the Hue and Cry.
HENRY TURNER . I was not examined before the Magistrate, or at all in this case.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know where your brother is? A. No—I heard from him last about a fortnight after this took place—he was then down in Somersetshire—I did not tell my master or the officer—I did not know they were searching for him—I went with them once to search for him at a place where they thought he was lodging, but he was not there—I could not tell where he was to be found—that was before I had the letter.
COURT. Q. Under what circumstances did he leave Mr. Godfrey? A. He was too long gone with goods on Greenwich fair day—Mr. Godfrey complained of it, and they parted—I never saw the prisoner till the day he was taken—I have been with Mr. Godfrey above four years.
MR. HICKS re-examined. When the prisoner came to me he was dressed in a leather apron up to his collar, with a strap of leather round, much like the dress worn by Mr. Godfrey's men—I asked him both if it was Mr. Godfrey's order, and if it was Mr. Godfrey's handwriting.
GUILTY . Aged 41.— Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18450512-1209]
1209. WILLIAM CHURCHILL was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of May, at Camberwell, 1 wooden till, value 2 s. 6 d.; and 3 pence; the property of Airth Mungo, in his dwelling-house; and afterwards, about the hour of one in the night, burglariously breaking out of the said dwelling-house.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM SMITH . I am potman to Mr. Airth Mungo, who keeps the George public-house in the Camberwell-road, in the parish of Camberwell. I fastened up the house on Sunday night, the 4th of May, about twelve o'clock, or twenty minutes after—I fastened the front door with an iron bar and a prop, and the back door in the same way—there is an inner door near the bar, leading to the private part of the house, that fastens with I bolt only, nearest to the bar—I bolted it—about ten minutes after one I was called by the police—I went down stairs, and found the till taken out of its place, and put on the counter—I had left it in its place under the counter, and a little copper money in it—I believe none was taken—the doors were all three unfastened and open—about eleven o'clock on the Sunday night I had seen three persons in the tap-room—I believe the prisoner to be one of them—I saw them come in and out—the last time I saw them was coming in—I did not go into the tap-room after that, and did not see them go out—when called by the police I observed some soot on the counter, and likewise about the stove in the tap-room—I saw a cap and a pair of shoes lying there—they were also sooty—a person could get from the back of the house over the garden fence, and so get away over an open piece of ground—the policeman has the shoes.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You did not take anything into the tap-room to them? A. No, I did not—I was at the bar, and could see them—our house is a kind of passage; people walk through all the day long; they use it as a thoroughfare into George-street, which leads into Southampton-street and into New Peckham—it is out of the way to Camberwell-green—I know Wells by sight—he comes in sometimes, like another customer—he lives just behind the house.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Did Wells come in that morning after the police were there? A. Yes, at one o'clock—he told them something—the soot appeared as if somebody had been up the chimney.
CHARLOTTE MUNGO . I am the wife of Airth Mungo, who keeps the I George. I saw Smith close the house about twelve o'clock—I went into the tap-room, and put the gas out—it was then, as near as possible, twenty minutes or a quarter to twelve—there were no persons in the tap-room then, to my knowledge—I looked under the places—I did not look up the chimney—I observed no soot there—I saw it afterwards—there was not much—might have been so when I was there, and I not observe it—there was no cap or shoes lying on the floor, and the tills were in their proper places—when I came down, after being disturbed, I found one of the tills on the counter, and a pair of shoes, a cap, and some soot in the tap-room.
WILLIAM HENRY CUNNINGHAM . I am a water-man or kind of ostler at the George. I was in the tap-room on Sunday night, the 4th of May, and saw the prisoner and two others there—they first came into the tap-room about half-past nine o'clock—I left at twenty minutes before eleven—the prisoner and the other two men were then there, by themselves.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you quite sure of that? A. Quite certain—they had one pot of beer—one of them brought it into the tap-room—nobody in particular waits on the customers—I do sometimes when I am there—I did not take any beer in to them—I was sitting against the door, and they were by the fire—I sat facing them—they sat there all the time—the man brought the beer in when he came in at first—they did not go out afterwards in my presence—they talked very little—the man with the beer came in behind the others—he was a tall man—I do not know hit name—I had seen him before—he was there three Sunday evenings before that—he was a dark-complexioned young man—I was at the police-office—I saw some of the prisoner's witnesses there—neither of them were the tall man that came in—I did not speak to the prisoner or either of the others—I was sleepy.
MR. PATNE. Q. Were there other persons in the tap-room besides those three men? A. No, nobody else, all the time, and I left them there.
ALEXANDER HOPPER ( police-constable P 183.) On Monday rooming, the 5th of May, at one o'clock, I was on duty in Camber well-road—I tried the door of the George public-house, and thought it rather gave in—I pressed against it again, and hallooed out to my brother constable that I did not consider it was all right—I heard a noise inside like a dog, or something shoved against the door—a bar, which was laid against it, fell, and I got in—I observed the till on the counter, with some coppers in it—the back door was unsecured, the bolts out—anybody could hare got out there while I was coming in at the front—I found a pair of shoes, one on the stove, and the other lying below, with soot, and a cap with soot on it—I found nobody there—Wells came in about twenty-five minutes after I had first gone in, and three policemen—from what Wells said to me I went in search of the prisoner, and about eleven o'clock on Monday evening I found him in the stable-loft in Waterloo-street, Camberwell, apparently asleep—I shook him, and asked if he slept there last night—he said yes, he did—a gentleman there said, " Bill, you know you did not"—he then said he did not—I asked him where he had been on the Sunday night—he said he had been at the Boyton Arms (which is a beer-shop on the banks of the canal) with two others; that the landlord closed up the house, (he did not mention the time;) from there he came down Southampton-street, and went to the Elephant and Castle, and had some coffee at a coffee-stall kept
by a female; that he emained there a consideranle time, and went from there to a public-house—as we were taking him away he said, "I know what it is for; it is about the George"—my brother constable brought him to the station—I took off the shoes which he had on, and put on the pair which I found in the prosecutor's house—he put one of them on—he turned them over, and said, "They are not my shoes," but the left shoe exactly fitted him—he only tried on one—I found marks in the graden adjoining the back of the prosecrtor's house, going from the back into George-street—I could not say whether they were the foot-marks of a person with or without shoes, the ground was so very hard.
Cross-examined. Q. It is a gravel-road, is it not? A. Yes—the footmarks were not on the road, but in the adjoining gardens—they came along the ditch—I cannot say how many persons' footmarks there were—Idid not go to the Elephant and Castle to look for a woman there—there is a woman sells coffee there.
NICHOLAS BUTLER ( police-constanle P 314.) On Sunday night, or Monday morning, I was on duty in Camberwell-road at a quarter after one o'clock, and about a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's house, I met the prisoner and two others coming in a direction from the prosecutor's house towards London—I observed that the prisoner had no shoes on, and no cap or hat—I took particular notice of him—I carry a light with me and used it—which means beat a constable or somebody—I folowed them about ten yards—they were walking three abreast, very fast—the prisoner and the one nearest to me were somking pipes—I have no doudt about the prosoner—I described him to my fellow-constable in the morning.
Cross-examined. Q. They walked pretty freely? A. They walked very fast, right by me—the prisoner spoke loud—Ihave heard the word malleting used before in the West of England, and in London as well, and different places, before I was in the police—I was them a bricklayer—it is used among bricklayers as well as others, and means the same thing—I had no grounds for stopping them then.
GEORGE ROUS ( police-constable P 219.) On the mirning of the 5th of may, about ten road, half-way between Camberwell-gate and the George—I was turinng to go into the square, about mid-way form Southampton-street to where my brother constable was, which was neared the gate, and saw three man pass me in a direction for Londom—I saw one of them without shoes—I did not see his face—I observed his feet when he had passed—I looked after him sufficient to see he was without shoes—I observed his height and genertal appearance, and have no doudt the prisoner is the man—I did not observe his head—he was outside the other two, and about half a step before them.
Cross-examined. Q. Was anybody with you? A. No—I did not observe that he was talking—I never rememner having seen him before—I did not observe whether he had a hat or cap on—I could hear him slouching as if without shoes—that induced me to turn my attention to him—he made a different noise to what he would if he had shoes—I could hear the noise—Butler was nearer to London than I was.
JAMES KEARNE ( police-constable P 36.) I assisted in taking the prisoner into custdy—he appeared to be asleep—I awoke him up—when he saw me he said, "I know what you want me for, it is the George affair"—
in coming along I asked him again whether he knew anything respecting the George—he said, "I will not answer that question."
Cross-examined. Q. Was your question to him, "Were you there?" A. It was, and he said, "I shall answer no questions"—it was in the loft when I aroused him, that he said, "I know what you want me for, it is the George affair," but it was in coming along I said, "Were you there?"—he did not say he was not there—I never heard him say so—this is my writing—( the deposition being read stated, "I said, 'Were you there?' he denied it; I afterwards asked him, and he said, 'I shall answer no questions' ")—he never did deny it—he said, "I shall answer you no questions"—he did say he was not there—hearing that read over has altered my recollection a little.
JAMES WELLS . I am a labourer, and live at No. 3, George-street, Camberwell-road, at the back of the George public-house. On Monday morning, the 5th of May, or the middle of Sunday night, I was awoke by the springing of a rattle—I got up and went out to see what was the matter—the policeman said, would I spring his rattle—I went and sprang it for other policemen to come—I then returned back to the George, and saw a pair of shoes on the bar—these aie them—I knew them to be the prisoners directly—I have known him about five months, to the best of my recollection—he slept in the loft of Mr. Bullock's stable, in Waterloo-street—about a quarter to five o'clock that same morning I went to that stable—he was not there—he was to come to work with me—he did not come during that day—I did not see him until five o'clock that evening, at the Waterloo Arms—he had an old pair of shoes on then—I said, "Bill, where are your prads" meaning shoes—he made no answer—I know the shoes produced—they are the shoes the prisoner gave to the shoemaker, or whatever he calls himself, to have an underlay put on the right shoe, and the upper-leather of the left was to be sewn up—I was present when he gave them to the shoemaker in the public-house on the Saturday week before the robbery—I saw before the Magistrate the shoes the prisoner had on then—they were not the shoes I had seen him give to the shoe-maker—I have seen him wearing these shoes every day—I am quite certain they are his shoes.
Cross-examined. Q. You swear these are the shoes that were given to the shoemaker in the public-house? A. Yes—I never got into any scrape but once, and that was nine years ago, about a few potatoes which I got from Mr. Tibbald's ground, a farmer at Peckham—I had to pay a fine for it— malleting means fighting—I live in a very respectable house—the landlady is a widow—I do not know her name—I keep the house—I am married—I was in bed when I heard the rattle spring—I went to the stable where the prisoner sleeps about a quarter to five to call him to come to work—that was my object, nothing else—there was no arrangement between us—I had then given information to the police that these shoes belonged to the prisoner—I did not tell the police then that I would show them where the prisoner was—I did about a quarter or twenty minutes before five—I told Hopper so—he and I went there together, and he was not there—I went with Hopper to have him apprehended if he had been there.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Was he to have worked with you that day? A. Yes, Mr. Rofy, my master, told me so—I made sure he would be at the stable at that time—the prisoner had worked for Mr. Rofy before, and was to do so that Monday.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Just point out what was done to shoes?—( handing him those found on the prisoner.) A. These are not the shoes—these are the shoes that were mended ( looking at the others)—the prisoner told the shoemaker to put a piece under the right shoe, and the left was to be soled and heeled—I heard all that was directed to be done, at the Waterloo Arms, on the Saturday week before this Sunday—I do not know the shoemaker's name, I know the man—I saw him at the police-office—it was George Powell—I am not a shoemaker.
Witnesses for the Defence.
GEORGE POWELL . I am sometimes a navigator, and sometimes make shoes. On Saturday, the 26th of April, I received a pair of shoes from I the prisoner to mend, at the Waterloo Arms—I have practised shoemaking I occasionally for the last ten years—these are not the shoes—( looking at those found in the prosecutor's house)—I should say they would very nearly fit the prisoner—I know they are not his—these are the shoes I repaired for him—they are the shoes I received at the public-house, when Wells was present—( those taken from the prisoner's feet)—I know them by the work, and by the pair of plain tips that I put on them with five holes in them, and the back lining of each shoe was sewn with a needle and thread, by my wife—I received no other shoes but these at the Waterloo Arms—they are different to the others—I had them on the Saturday, and I took them to the prisoner's on the Sunday morning, about half-past eleven o'clock—these other shoes I never saw before I was at the police-office—they are a similar cut shoe to the prisoner's—those I mended are Oxonians—I should not say the others would fit the prisoner—I never saw any of his shoes like these—he had on an old pair of blucher boots when I had these—he had no others to my knowledge—I hare known him quite well, and seen him frequently—this cap is not his—I never saw him wear a cap like it—he generally wears a blue cloth cap with a peak to it—( looking at one produced by the prisoner)—this is the cap he has generally worn—I never knew of his having one like the other.
MR. PAYNE. Q. How old are you? A. Twenty-five—I have known the prisoner pretty well all my life—I have mended shoes for him a great many times—I never mended a pair of oxonians for him before these—I will not swear the other shoes will not fit him—I should not say they would lit him as the others do—I never saw them tried on—I live at No. 6, Byatt's-buildings, George-street, Camberwell, not far from the George—it was between five and six o'clock on the Saturday that the prisoner gave me the shoes to mend—a fellow-servant, named Field, who works with the water-casks, was there, and Wells—I took them home to my own house about six o'clock—I mended them on Sunday morning from nine o'clock till about half-past eleven, when I took them home—there have been times when I have not seen the prisoner, when I have been at work, at no other time—I swear that—three months, I believe, is the longest time I have lost sight of him—he lived in Cork-street—for the last six months he has slept in a stable—I did not see him there, visit him at his house, or work with him, but I have worked for him—I have been to his father-in-law's, in Cork-street, where he did live—he has not lived there for the last six months—I have seen him at the Waterloo Arms, where he mostly had his meals.
SARAH POWELL . I am the wife of last witness. I assisted him in
mending a pair of shoes of the prisoner's on Sunday morning, the 27th of April—these are them—I sewed the back seam with black thread, and sewed the binding on—I know my work—these other shoes I have never seen—they have never been in our house, to my knowledge—my husband has been in the habit of mending for the prisoner for a considerable time-only myself and my husband were in the house at the time the shoes were mended.
MR. PAYNE. Q. You were not before the Magistrate, I believe? A. I was not—my husband had these shoes on the Saturday, and returned them on the Sunday—that was the only occasion on which I mended any shoes for the prisoner—I took no particular notice of them at the time—I have been married twelve months—I knew the prisoner before I was married.
MR. PRENDEROAST. Q. Do you perfectly well know the time you mended them? A. On the Sunday morning, the 27th of April—I am sure that was the day—I have seen the prisoner wearing a cap, but never one of this colour or description—there is another witness here who saw the shoes.
JOSEPH COOK . I saw the prisoner with a pair of shoes on Dun'aay, the 4th of May, the same Sunday this happened—it was a pair of oxonians—the left one was torn towards the toe in front—this is it—these are the shoes—they had tips—I saw them after they were mended, before he had them on—the last witness is my sister.
MR. PAYNE. Q. What are you? A. A shoemaker—I was not before the Magistrate—I was first applied to to speak about this matter the week before last, by Mr. Powell—I saw the prisoner on this Sunday standing outside Mr. Bolter's, the Waterloo Arms—he put up his feet to show Powell that he wanted his shoes mended—Powell said if he would let him have them on Monday he would do them—this was about half-past four o'clock in the afternoon—I had called to see my sister and brother-in-law, and we went out for a walk in the afternoon, and there saw the prisoner—I have known him about six months—I knew he had had a pair mended the week before—I was at Powell's, and saw them before and after they were mended—they were only tipped and heeled, and he had worn the soles out and cut the front against a stone—they were the same shoes—I live in Boss-street, James-street, Horsleydown—I work on my own account—I used to work for a shop in the City—I have not had much work lately—I am very seldom with the prisoner—I law him with these shoes on twice—Powell had shown me what he had to do to the shoes, and when I saw the prisoner with them on, after they had been mended he showed me what had been done to them, and said he had made a good job of them.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you ever see him wear a cap at all like that? A. No—he wore a blue cap.
NEWHALL. I am the prisoner's father-in-law. I know his cap—this is not it—he never wore such a thing—he generally wore a blue cap with a peak to it, like this—my attention has not been directed to his shoes—I have looked after a coffee woman at the Elephant and Cattle, but have not been able to find her.
MR. PAYNE. Q. What are you? A. A bricklayer—the prisoner has not lived with me for some months past—as I had two daughters grown up he said he would go out and get lodgings—I believe he slept in a bed in
the stable—I saw him most every day—I was before the Magistrate, and gave my account to him, but was not sworn.
JOHN DAVIS . I keep the Boyton Arms beer-shop, near the canal in New Church-road, Camberwell. The church bell-ringers practise the art of bell-ringing—we have a peal of seventeen bells for their practice—it is a society held in our club-room—I saw the prisoner at my house—I cannot say what time he came there—I saw him at half-past eleven o'clock, when he came down stairs to go away, and I saw him some short time before, between ten and o'clock I should think, sitting down with two others—he made a remark to me, which is the reason why I know him—he was in (be club-room where the bells were—it is open on Sunday evenings—I wu before the Magistrate—inspector Giffbrd sent the officers to know whether what the prisoner had stated as to being in my house was correct, and at I identified him they did not swear me.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Was the prisoner in the habit of frequenting your beer-shop? A. No—I never saw him there but once before—there were very likely twenty or thirty persons in the room that night—I am not aware that there is anybody else here from my house—I did not see the prisoner after half-past eleven o'clock—I should say it was rather more than that—my house is about half a mile from the George, I suppose, but I never saw the house to my knowledge.
---- LESTER. I am a bricklayer. I was at the Boyton Arms on the Sunday night of the robbery—I saw the prisoner there at half-past eight o'clock—he remained there till half-past eleven—I then left—I left the prisoner outside the door along with two more.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you often go to the Boyton Arms? A. I do generally on a Sunday—I am one of the bell-ringers—I only saw the prisoner there once, and that was that night—I knew him before—I know this was on Sunday night, the 4th of May, a fortnight last Sunday—he asked me to drink twice—I did not see him after half-past eleven o'clock—I was not examined before the Magistrate.
JAMES HISTEY . I was examined before the Magistrate last Tuesday fortnight—I was at the Boyton Arms with the prisoner on the Sunday night when this burglary is said to have been committed—(I heard of bit being taken up on the Tuesday)—he remained there from eight o'clock till half-past eleven—I was with him all that time—we then went to the Anchor and Hope, and from there to the Elephant and Castle, to get some coffee—we got some—I was with the prisoner till half-past two on the Monday morning—I never saw him wear iuch a cap as this—he wore a blue cap—this is not my cap—I do not know whose it is—I never saw the house to my knowledge.
MR. PAYNE. Q. He did not generally wear such a cap? A. No, he never wore such a cap since I knew him—I had a bed to go to, and so had he—I cannot say why I did not go home—he gave me no reason for not going home—I left him at half-past two at the Elephant and Castle with Barton—they were drinking coffee—we were not doing anything besides drinking coffee—only standing against the Elephant and Castle—we did not get there till a few minutes before one—we had been drinking at the Boyton Arms from eight till half-past eleven—we had ale and beer—we then went to Mr. Reed's, the Anchor and Hope, in Southampton-street—that is not a great way from the George—the bottom of George-street opens into Southampton-street—we were not more than five or ten minutes
getting there—we staid at the Anchor and Hope about four or five minutes—we had half-a-pint of gin and cloves there—we went from there to the Elephant and Castle—I took no notice of the time, but the clock struck one very shortly after we had been standing there, because Barton pulled out his watch, and showed us—I had no pipe—I swear that.
Q. On your oath, did not you go into the George between the'time you left the Anchor and Hope, and your going to the Elephant and Castle? A. No, I swear I did not go near the George—we were doing nothing between leaving the Anchor and Hope, and going to the Elephant and Castle—we walked right along the road—I cannot say how long it took us, but I can swear we never went in anywhere after we left the Anchor and Hope—we went from the Duke of Clarence at the corner of Southampton-street right away up to the Elephant and Castle, straight up the Wai worth-road—the prisoner had snoes on—that I swear—Barton had on a white jacket, a pair of moleskin trowsers, and a cap—I will not be certain whether I had a pipe—I do not think I had—I believe the prisoner had a pipe—he had a cap on—I swear that—and his shoes also—I heard nothing said about malleting anybody—we saw a policeman against the Duke of Clarence—I said nothing to him, but Barton did—I do not know what he said—I cannot swear which policeman it was—I did not see another policeman near Addington-square as we were going along.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Had you got any young ladies that night t A, Not in our company—there was one came in with another party.
---- BARTON. I am a bricklayer's labourer. I was at the Boyton Arms on this night—I had some money—the others had a little—I had had a pretty middling week—we went to hear the bell-ringing—we got there about eight o'clock, and stopped till about half-past eleven or a quarter to twelve—the prisoner was in my company all the time—neither of us went into the George between eight and half-past eleven—I am quite certain of that—after leaving the Boyton Arms we went to the Anchor and Hope, and then to the Elephant and Castle, where we had some coffee of a woman—about half-past two o'clock in the morning Histed went away—I and the prisoner stopped at the Elephant and Castle and had some more coffee—I remained in his company till seven o'clock next morning—during the whole of that time he was not in the George at all—I parted with him at Mr. Rofy's, where he works, opposite the Waterloo Arms, Camberwell—we went through the George to get there—it is a thoroughfare—I have known the prisoner as long as I have known myself—I know he had his shoes on while he was in my company—I do not know what particular shoes he had on—this cap is not his—I never saw him with a cap at all like it—I never saw it before that I am aware of—I went before the Magistrate to give evidence about the prisoner's being with me—I was sworn.
MR. PAYNE. Q. You left the Boyton Arms about half-past eleven? A. Yes, or a quarter to twelve—I will not be sure to a quarter of an hour—we were only a few minutes at the Anchor and Hope, just to stop and drink a glass of gin and cloves—I suppose we got to the Elephant and Castle a little before one—I heard the clock strike while I was there—it took us all that time to walk from the Anchor and Hope—we staid at the Elephant and Castle till half-past two, drinking coffee and eating bread and butter, and longer than that—I cannot say how much we had, or how much I paid—we were not trying people's doors—after drinking the coffee we went and had some more beer at a public-house just opposite St.
George's Church, on the same side of the way—we got there a little before four, as soon as it was open—we stopped at the coffee-stall from one till near four, drinking coffee and eating bread and butter—the prisoner sometimes works at Mr. Rofy's—he ought to have been at work there that Monday—he did not go home all night—he was along with me—we were drinking beer at the public-house till about half-past five, and then came back again to Camberwell—we got back at seven—no one was in our company at the public-house—I believe I was smoking a pipe as we came along the Camberwell-road to the Elephant and Castle—I cannot tell whether the prisoner was smoking or not—we met a policeman—he did not turn his light on to look at us—that I swear—we met him at the corner of Southampton-street, near the Duke of Clarence—we did not meet one near the gate.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you speak to the policeman? A. Yes—it was Hopper—I changed half-a-sovereign at the Waterloo Arms in the evening—I had 2 s. or 3 s. left in the morning.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Were you alone, or was anybody with you when you saw and spoke to that policeman? A. The other two went on—I did not ask the policeman if he had seen a young man pass, dressed like a brick-layer, whom I called Bill —I met this policeman on the Saturday night, and asked him that question, but not on the Sunday night—I spoke to him, but what I said I do not know—it was at the corner just against the Duke of Clarence, about five or ten minutes past twelve.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you ever see the prisoner in such a cap as that? A. Never, and these shoes are not his—I never observed him with any like them.
COURT. Q. Where did he go to his work at seven o'clock in the morning? A. To Mr. Rofy's, in George-street—he worked there regularly in the watering time—I know Wells—I do not know whether it was at the same place where Wells was to go to work.
MR. PAYNE. Q. You do not mean to say that he did work at Mr. Rofy's that day? A. No, he did not—he went with the intent to do so—I saw him afterwards on the Monday—I did no work that day—we spent the greater part of the day at the Waterloo Arms.
---- REED. I am landlord of the Anchor and Hope. I have no knowledge of the prisoner, but the last witness was at my house, with two other young men and two females, from twelve o'clock to perhaps ten minutes before twelve—I will not say exactly the time—they had half a pint of gin and cloves, for which Barton paid—they looked clean, particularly Barton—he looked very clean—neither of them looked as if they had come out of a chimney—I think they had caps on, and they were smoking—Barton lighted his pipe at my gas before he went out.
---- BOLTER. I keep the Waterloo Arms, in George-street. The stable where the prisoner slept belongs to Mr. Bullock—a young man and he slept together in a loft over the stable—I saw the prisoner on the Monday morning, about seven o'clock—he had a cap on, and I think I should have observed if he had been without shoes—he did not look as if he had just come out of a chimney—he was dressed as usual—he was in the habit of assisting my man to clean my pots, and I reprimanded him well for not being there to do so—Mr. Rofy is a cornchandler, opposite, and contracts for watering the roads—I never saw the prisoner in such a cap as this—he usually wore a blue cap.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Where does he live? A. I cannot say—he jobs about for most people in the neighbourhood, and sleeps at Mr. Bullock's loft—I was never up there—it is at the side of my house—they have a bed—I do not know the name of the young man that sleeps with him—it was. neither of the witnesses.
---- NEWHALL re-examined. I have got the shirt here that the prisoner took off that morning—it is very dirty, but not the least stain of soot upon it—he came and changed it at my house on the Monday morning—I have kept it ever since without washing it, as they said he was found in the chimney.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you know this loft over the stable? A. Yes—I have been at work there—they had a rug and things to sleep upon—the man that looks after Mr. Bullock's horses and the prisoner slept there together.
MR. PAYNE recalled
NICHOLAS BUTLER . I would not swear that Histey and Burton were the two men who were with the prisoner, but I believe Burton, from his appearance, to be one of the men—I turned my light on his face—he afterwards owned to me that he did pass me, and that he touched me on the right elbow as he passed me.
GEORGE ROUS . I do not recollect either Histey or Burton.
NOT GUILTY .

1210. GEORGE SHARP was indicted for stealing 67 yards of calico, value 1 l. 5 s., the goods of James Worley.
JAMES WORLEY . I am a woollen-draper, and live in Surrey-place, Newington Butts. About nine o'clock, on Saturday morning, the 3rd of May, I saw the prisoner come into my shop, take a piece of calico, put it under his arm, and walk off with it—I followed him, and called," Stop thief!"—I saw him drop it, and picked it up—it is my property.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you see it taken? A. I did—I was at the desk, in the middle of the shop—I could see the prisoner, but he could not see me—I am quite sure the person who took it was the person who threw it down.
GUILTY .** Aged 19.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.

1212. CHARLES RENDALL was indicted for stealing 971bs. of hay, value 4 s., the property of Henry Perkins, his master; and WILLIAM WOODLAND for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the statute, &c.
JOHN COOPER . I am bailiff to Mr. Henry Perkins, of Hanworth-park, near Hounslow—on the 30th of April Rendall came there with some oats from Messrs. Barclay and Perkins's brewery—he is their drayman—the oats were all right—I took them in and they were put into the granary—there
could not have been two trusses of hay in the wagon then without my seeing them—Mr. Perkin's hay was kept in the loft in the farm-yard—I saw two trusses of hay afterwards at Wandsworth, which looked very much like that my master had in the loft—I did not give any hay to Rendall—no one was authorised to do so.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. What time did Rendall come with the oats? A. About eleven o'clock—I was a little way from home—he told me he had waited about half an hour, he could not unload till I came home—Mr. Perkin's farm is about 400 acres, and has sometimes a good many persons about.
THOMAS THOMPSON . I am a labourer in the service of Mr. Perkins—on the 30th of April I saw Rendall in the farm-yard, between one and two o'clock—I saw him take a truss of hay up from the ground and put it in his wagon—there was no other hay in the wagon at that time—I then went to dinner and when I came out I noticed the wagon again—there was then two trusses of hay in it—his wagon was not above eight or ten yards from the hay-loft—I saw the hay which was afterwards brought from Wands-worth, and it was very similar to what Mr. Perkins had—I made the rick myself and had used a great deal of it.
JOHN FIELDER . I am a labourer and live in Gray's-inn-place, Holborn—I was a stranger to Rendall, but I overtook him driving a wagon near the Old Ship at Richmond on the 30th of April—he gave me a ride in his wagon, and after I had been in a few minutes he asked me to drive the horses and hold the reins—he went to sleep on the wagon—I moved the tarpaulin and saw some hay under it—I went on to the Plough at Wands-worth—we got out there to have a pot of beer—we got there about four o'clock, and remained about a quarter of an hour—when I came out of the house I saw Rendall talking to Woodland who is the under ostler there—they were standing under a shelter outside the house where persons sit down—I went to the wagon again and found the tarpaulin was lying down flat, and what it had covered was gone—I went into the tap-room again and soon after Daley, the officer, came in—Rendall was sitting alongside of me—the officer asked for Barclay and Perkins's drayman—Rendall said nothing, but the landlord came in and pointed him out—the officer asked Rendall if he had any hay on his wagon—he said he had none—the officer said he must go out and see about it.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. You were sitting in the wagon driving? A. Yes, when I got up into the wagon Rendall went to sleep—I saw some hay when I happened to lift the tarpaulin—I remained io my seat—I could see into the road from the tap-room.
HENRY ORGER . I am a tallow-chandler and live at Putney. On the 30th of April, about five o'clock in the afternoon, I was going towards the Plough public-house, at Wandsworth—I saw Barclay and Perkins's wagon standing before the Plough—I saw Woodland remove the tarpaulin and take from the wagon something bulky, which appeared to be hay—he took it into the stable—I walked on till I was opposite the wagon—I saw him return and take a second truss of hay from the wagon—he placed them one on the other in the first stall in the stable, and he locked the stable door—the horses were not in the stable, they were in the wagon—I took the number of the wagon, and I met Daley, the officer—I then accompanied him to the Plough and pointed out Woodland as the person who had removed the hay—Daley asked him where the hay was that he had taken
from the wagon—he made a deal of shuffling—he first said he knew nothing about it, and afterwards he said he would tell the truth, there was the hay, and he brought it out—on the way to the station he said, "I will tell the truth, I took the hay from the wagon by order of the wagoner"—Daley told him to hold his tongue, he wanted to hear nothing from him, and what he said he should be obliged to repeat in evidence—Daley brought Rendall out of the public-house and asked him what he had done with the hay that was on his wagon—he said, "Hay, what hay? I know nothing about any hay"—Daley told him he could prove he had hay—he said he had no hay—Daley told him if he denied it he must arrest him, and he must cottager himself in custody—the two prisoners were taken to the station.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. What are you? A. A tallow-chandler—I live in Gardener's-lane, Putney. I am in the employ of Willshire and Son, of Crucifix-lane, Bermondsey—the greater part of my time I am out getting orders and collecting money—I work in the factory, and am partly a town traveller—I have been there since the latter end of Nov.—I have collected outstanding debts for respectable gentlemen, and when they have been sued for, I have served the process.
THOMAS DALEY ( police-constable V 127.) I received information from Orger—I went to the Plough—I saw the wagon, with "Barclay and Perkins" on it—I first spoke to Woodland—I asked him what he had done with the hay which he took off the wagon—he made no answer—I asked him a second time—he said nothing, but went to go to the public-house—I stopped him, and said, "I want that hay you took out of the wagon, and put into the stable"—he opened the stable door, and gave it me outr—I then went into the tap-room, and saw Rendall—I asked for Barclay and Perkins's man—Rendall made no answer, but the landlord pointed him out—I then asked what he had done with'the hay he had on the wagon—he said, "I had none on"—I said, "Come out and see"—I brought him to the wagon, and said, "Do you mean positively to say you had no bay on this wagon?"—he said, "I do"—when we were going away I said, "Don't say anything to each other, for if you do, I am bound to produce it in evidence"—Woodland then said, "Old man, I want a word with you; you ought not to have taken all the hay, it looks so much"—I then cautioned him again, and he said, "I will tell the troth; I did take it off the wagon, but it was by order of the wagon, pointing to Rendall, who was in custody of the other officer.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. How many persons were in the tap-room? A. I think about four—I believe they were drinking and talking—Fielder and Rendall sat together.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. Do you know how long Woodland has been ostler there? A. He has been under-ostler, I think, for nine or ten years—I have been on duty in that vicinity for fifteen years—I never heard anything against him.
DAVID FERNIE . I am a haybinder, in Mr. Perkins's service—I have seen the two trusses of hay which were found at the Plough—they were my binding, and Mr. Perkins's property—these are the bands—they are my making—I do not work for anybody else—I can tell my own binding from any other.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Are these anything more than hay twisted round and round into the shape of a roller? A. No.
JURY. Q. Do you ever leave the bands, that they may be taken away before you put them on the trusses? A. I never leave any, except they are on the trusses.
JOHN COOPER re-examined. The hay was under my charge—the loft was never kept locked—I have not missed any hay.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
WOODLAND — GUILTY . Aged 57.
RENDALL — GUILTV . Aged 27.
Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18450512-1213]
1213. JANE TURNER was indicted for stealing 1 table-cover, value 4 s. 6 d., the goods of John Askew.
JOHN ASKEW . I carry on business at No. 110, Blackman-street, Borough. On the evening of the 18th of April I was standing on the stain, and saw the prisoner sitting by the side of my counter—she took a table-cover from the counter, folded it, and put it under her shawl—I came down and waited about two minutes—she was sitting by the side of the counter—I told my man to go for the policeman—she then took the cover from under her shawl, and said, "What, about this"—she said she was going to ask the price of it—she had not bought anything then.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. The person who was with her did buy something? A. Yes, and it was higher in price than this table-cover, it was a hearth-rug, which came to about 5 s. or 6 s.—I cannot say what the prisoner's intention might be—she brought the cover out at once, as soon as I talked about the policeman—I believe her friends are very respectable—I believe it was her sister who was with her—the prisoner is not married, her sister is—I said, "These people don't want to buy a hearth-rug," and they had not bought one then, but after I sent for the policeman they bought one and paid for it—I think I have stated all that happened—I think the prisoner might be tempted to take this cover, from being placed in favourable circumstances for so doing, as the young men were out of the way, except the person who was serving the rug—after I had told the young man to go for the policeman, the prisoner said she wanted to ask the price of the cover—I told her, and she said it was too much—I was willing to sell it, but she would not pay the price.
COURT. Q. Do you mean that after you charged her with stealing it; she asked the price? A. Yes, and I said, "You don't want to buy one;" but she persisted in asking the price—I said it was 6 s.—she said it was too much—it had cost me less than the rug did.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18450512-1214]
1214. JOHN JONES was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 3 l., the goods of James Brooks; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
ANN BROOKS . I am the wife of James Brooks; we live in Devonshire-street, Lambeth. On the 19th of Nov. the prisoner, whom I knew before, came and dined with me—I went out, and asked him to remain while I went out—I came back in about ten minutes, and he was gone—I heard something, and went into the back parlour—my husband's watch was gone, which had been safe when I went away—this is it.
EMILY SNELGROVE . I saw the prisoner come out of Mrs. Brooks's
house—he had the watch in his hand—I saw the chain, and knew it was Mr. Brooks's.
JOHN MELVILLE . This watch was pawned with me on the 20th of Nov., I believe by the prisoner.

1215. CATHERINE GREEN was indicted for stealing 1 gown, the goods of Alice Barber.
ALICE BARBER . I am a widow, and live at Rotherhithe. I left my gown behind the door on the 5th of April, between three and four o'clock in the afternoon, leaving the door locked—I returned on the Monday following—I found the door open, and the gown gone—the prisoner was not at home—I lodge there, and her husband belongs to the house—when she came home I asked her where my dark gown was—she said she took it and pawned it, and would get it me again.
Prisoner. She lent me the gown. Witness. I did not.
JOHN WADHAM . I am a pawnbroker. This gown was pawned on the 5th of April by the prisoner, in the name of Ann Davis, which is the name she goes by.
Prisoner. She is my husband's own aunt.

1216. THOMAS REDDY was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Askew, about the hour of three in the night of the 17th of April, in St. George the Martyr, Southwark, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 2 coats, value 2 l. 10 s.; 1 half-sovereign, 3 shillings, 9 groats, 36 pence, and 24 halfpence; his property.
JOHN ASKEW . I am a draper, and live at No. 110, Blackman-street, in the parish of St. George the Martyr, Southwark. I left my coat hanging in the counting-house at ten o'clock on Thursday night, the 17th of April—there was a letter in the pocket—the coat and letter now produced are what I left there—I was not the last up that night, nor the first up the next morning—I looked at the skylight over the counting-house the next morning, and part of the frame of it was broken—they had first broken a square, and opened a small light, and then opened the skylight, and got in—I missed the coat which had the letter in it.
JOHN BROWN . I am shopman to the prosecutor. I saw the skylight safe on the night of the 17th of April at ten o'clock—I was the last person in the shop—I locked the shop, and all was safe—there was a half-sovereign, three shillings, nine groats, thirty-six pence, and twenty-four bal£ pence in the till—I awoke in the morning about six o'clock, and went down a few minutes after—I then found the skylight broken—they had entered the shop, and taken the money from the till, and I missed two coats from the counting-house—all the drawers in the counting-house had been broken open, and the papers were all about the floor.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Were there many things stolen? A. Only the coats and money.
CHARLES CHAMBERS ( City police-constable, No. 523.) I was on duty in Lime-street at half-past five o'clock on the morning of the 18th of April—I saw the prisoner in company with another man—I saw the prisoner had a coat on which was too large for him—the other man ran away—I went and took the prisoner—I asked him where he got that coat—he said he bought it four or five months ago in Petticoat-lane—I took him to the station—the inspector found this letter in the pocket of the coat—this is the coat he had on.
STEPHEN MARTIN ( police-constable M 98.) I saw the prisoner about ten minutes past one o'clock in the morning on the 18th of April—he passed me—he had not this coat on then, and was going towards the prosecutor's house.
GUILTY .— Aged 19.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18450512-1217]
1217. GEORGE DEAN was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 4 s., the goods of George Gray, from his person.
GEORGE GRAY . I live in Holy well-street, Milbank. On the 20th of April, between four and five o'clock in the afternoon, I was in Battersea-fields—I saw a young man struck down by a horse—I approached the crowd, and I had not remained above a minute or two before I felt something at my pocket—I turned, and saw a lad with my handkerchief in his hand—I endeavoured to recover it from him, but in doing so he dropped it and got away—I gave an alarm—I am sure it was my handkerchief—I could not swear it was the prisoner who dropped it—I had only a glance at his face—I believe he is the person—he was exactly like him in size and clothing.
WILLIAM BROWN . I live in Bridge-road, Battersea, and am a carpenter. I was in the crowd, and saw the prisoner draw a handkerchief between me and Mr. Gray—I am sure it was the prisoner—he stood behind me and Mr. Gray—I saw his hand draw from between me and Mr. Gray—the prisoner turned round, and Mr. Gray turned and laid hold of him by the cuff of his coat—I saw the handkerchief drop from the prisoner—I am sure it dropped from him, and he ran away.
Prisoner. Q. Where were you standing? A. By the side of Mr. Gray.
Prisoner. You know it is false, I was lying down on the grass; this man came and took me.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Three Months.

1220. THOMAS LILLYWALL was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of Joseph Hunt, from his person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.

1221. ELIZABETH WATSON was indicted for stealing 1 purse, value 3 s.; 3 sovereigns, and 5 shillings; the monies of John Taylor, from his person.
JOHN TAYLOR . I live in Salisbury-crescent, Walworth. On the 30th of April, about ten or eleven o'clock at night, I was in the Dover-road, on my way home—the prisoner addressed me, and asked me to give her a glass of ale—in order to get rid of her I consented, as I had been annoyed on similar occasions—she wished me to go to a public-house which I did not like the appearance of—I said I would give it her at another house—we crossed the road for that purpose, and she was leading me a way I did not like, and wished to get away—I turned and saw two men who were following us, the prisoner being close to me, and speaking vociferously tome—one of the men then passed very close, and thrust the prisoner against me—the men then went off, one one way, and the other the other—immediately after I said, "I must go," and the prisoner let go of my arm—I put my hand to feel for my purse and watch, and I missed my purse which contained three sovereigns and some silver, which I had seen safe about two hours before.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was there not something tender going on? A. No—her cheek was close to mine—I kept her about ten minutes, and then gave her into custody—I believe there was only 2 1/2 d. found on the prisoner—I was sober—lam not married—I was walking with the prisoner for ten minutes or a quarter of an hour—she had hold of me.
WILLIAM DAVIS ( police-constable M 125). I took the prisoner—there was nothing found on her but 2 1/2 d.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18450512-1222]
1222. WILLIAM LEWIN was indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering a forged receipt for money, with intent to defraud John Oakley.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN OAKLEY . I am a black-lead manufacturer. The prisoner was in my employ as a packer—on the 11th of March I sent him to Mr. March, for some labels—he was to pay for them—I gave him a sovereign and j written order to get them—on the following morning he produced the labels with this bill and receipt, with Mr. March's name to it—I should have required a similar receipt to this from him on his return.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. What was the nature of the business he had to do? A. To pack black-lead at my house—it was piece-work—I paid him every Saturday night—the course was to make out an account for the work he had done in the week.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had he any right to appropriate this money for wages? A. No; he brought his bill in every Saturday night, and was paid.
JAMES MARCH . The prisoner came to me, on the 11th of March, and brought an order from his master, in consequence of which I delivered him 15,000 labels—I did not receive any money from him fort hem—I knew him as the servant of Mr. Oakley, as he had been before—the upper part of this bill is mine, "15,000 bills, 18 s. 9 d." but this "Paid, J. March," is not mine, nor did I authorize anybody to write it.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe it was paid shortly afterwards? A. Yes, the prisoner called on the 4th of April, and paid it—he has been in the habit of getting things before on three or four occasions, and on one occasion he paid for some goods which he had had when he gave orders for others.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 3 7.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined
Two Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.

1225. JAMES YALLOP was indicted for stealing 1 loaf of bread, value 6 d.; and 41bs. weight of flour, 7 d.; the goods of William Bush, his master.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM BUSH . I live in York-row, Walworth. In 1843 I carried on the business of a baker—I ceased to do so on the 31st of Dec. 1844.—I was brought up a farmer—the prisoner came into my service in 1843—he was to bake bread for me—it was part of his duty to carry out bread to my customers, but not without my leave—he had a guinea a-week, and a quartern of bread a-day, and a quartern of flour on Saturday nights—he continued in my service till the 25th of April, 1844—he never had his
bread before six o'clock on other nights, and from eight till ten on Saturday nights—on other'nights he left work a little after six, and on Saturday nights at ten—the bread he had was stale, and he had the flour on Saturday nights, from eight till ten o'clock, in a bag with my name marked on it—he generally had a baking of his own on Sundays, and always a batter-pudding under the meat—the bag that he had the flour I in on Saturday night was produced on Sunday over the meat—Mrs. Hunt made a communication to me, in consequence of which the prisoner was given into custody—I did not allow him a halfpenny a loaf on bread that he sold—I never gave him permission to open accounts with persons without my leave—I was not aware that any bread was being supplied to Mrs. Hunt—the prisoner left my service on the 25th of April, 1844—a day or two afterwards I found four quarterns of bread in a pump, mildewed—I had no other servant.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You were utterly surprised at Mrs. Hunt's information? A. I did not suspect anything of the kind—the prisoner lived with me about eight months—I do not know that he had been a master baker—I have heard say so—I gave him no right at all to sell any bread to Mrs. Hunt.
MARTHA HUNT . I am the wife of Edward Hunt. We live in George-street—I have known the prisoner about nine yean—in the beginning of Sept., 1843, he said Mr. Bush allowed him a halfpenny a loaf for the bread he sold, which made it better for Mr. Bush and for him, and he asked us to take bread of him—my husband said it did not matter who he had hit bread of, as he paid for it—we had not dealt with Mr. Bush before, nor did we know him—from that time we dealt with the prisoner—we had sometimes a quartern and a half of bread in a day, sometimes less, about six or seven loaves in a week—I took more on a Saturday than other days—I always had new bread—he brought the bread in a morning, and on Saturday, between four and five o'clock, he brought a quartern of flour—he continued to supply me from Sept. till the 26th of April following—these are the bills—on the 13th of April here is a charge for a loaf of bread and a quartern of flour—I had them of the prisoner—for about the first three weeks I paid for the bread and things as I had them—the prisoner then wished me to discontinue it—he said it would serve Mr. Bush, and he would have the overplus for himself—from the time I began to deal with him till I ceased, I never saw Mr. Bush—I paid the prisoner on account 1 l., and 3 s., and 8 s., and 3 s. again—last Friday week the prisoner had summoned my husband to the Court of Requests—I had not the money to pay it, and I went to Mr. Bush to see if I could talk to him to intercede with the prisoner to get time—the prisoner had summoned us in his own name.
Cross-examined. Q. There is an execution out against your husband, is there not? A. There is—I had known the prisoner nine years—he had been our tenant, and lived next door but one to us all the time this was going on—I have paid him three sums on this account since he left Mr. Bush—the rest he summoned us for—my husband went before the Commissioners, and they ordered execution—I then went to Mr. Bush to intercede with the prisoner—my husband is not out of the way—our house is not shut up—they have not been trying to take our goods under the execution—they have not issued process—our door is not kept shut—I
did not go up to the Court—the bread was brought stale once or twice not more.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Is your husband here? A. Yes—the floury brought in a bag on Saturdays between four and five o'clock—the bag on left with the flour—the name of William Bush, and a bunch of wheat was on it—a quartern of flour was brought—the prisoner left these bills himself—I saw him write when I gave him the sovereign.
WILLIAM MORTON ( police-constable P 289.) I took the prisoner into custody on the 29th of April—I told him he was taken on a charge of felony on account of Mr. Bush—he said, "A charge of felony! I will soon settle that."
Witnesses for the Defence.
WILLIAM TROUGHT . I live in East-lane, Walworth, and am a green-grocer—I have known the prisoner for years—he has been a baker—I was a baker in 1843 and 1844—I supplied him with bread from the 11th of Sept. till the 23rd of April, 1844—I allowed him a halfpenny a loaf profit—I have supplied him, according to my recollection, with a couple of French loaves and a quartern of flour a week, which I left at his bout—it was on such terms as would enable him to sell it—it is a general custom of bakers to give their men bread—the prisoner has children that are bakers.
COURT. Q. Do you know Mrs. Hunt? A. No—I have heard she lives in George-street—she did not live nearer to me than to Mr. Bush, but it would be handier for her to get a supply from me—I served two customers in the same street.
JOHN STANFIELD . I live in Earl-street, London-road, and am a baker—I have supplied the prisoner as a baker during 1843 and 1844—I have frequently supplied him with bread in my rounds, sometimes four times a week.
COURT. Q. What do you mean by your rounds? A. I mean my bat where I go round to my customers—he was employed within the limits of my rounds—when he was first in the employ, he met me and asked me several times whether I could let him have a few loaves, and I let bin have it at a price to sell it again—he might get a penny a loaf, but always a halfpenny—a halfpenny is what we allow to persons we know nothing of to sell again, but, knowing him, I would willingly allow him a penny a loaf.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. When did this supply take place? A. It began about this time two years as near as I can guess—I have no books here—I wanted no books, it was ready money—we never book it—I supply customers near to the prosecutor four times a week, on Monday, Wednesday, Friday, and Saturday—I met the prisoner several times, and he took a few loaves—I should say he had about two loaves a week.
NOT GUILTY .

1228. KEZIAH EDWARDS and DANIEL SULLIVAN were indicted for stealing 1 half-crown, 14 shillings, and 6 sixpences, the monies of John Rogers, from his person.
JOHN ROGERS . I keep a beer-shop in Red Cross-street. I had a booth at Wandsworth Fair—on the evening of the 13th of May, about ten o'clock* both the prisoners were in my booth—Sullivan asked me for a pint of beer, which he paid for—he had had four pints in the day, which he had not paid for—I gave him some meat, and Edwards some bread and cheese—she said she was Sullivan's cousin, and where he went she would go, but the would rather he would stay there, as he was tipsy, which he did—at eleven o'clock the officers came round and turned all out—the prisoners came in again about twelve, and Sullivan laid on a place about eight feet from me—Edwards said she would sit up all night—I then went to sleep on my left side, having in my watch-pocket 25 s. in silver, amongst which was a new half-crown and a remarkable sixpence—about half-past six o'clock the policeman came and awoke me, to let him have a pint of beer—I got up and drew it—I laid down again, and when I awoke again I found part of my money was gone, and Sullivan was gone—he was there when I went to sleep, after serving the policeman—Edwards was in the booth—I taxed her with it—she said she knew nothing about it, but after a good while she said it might be Sullivan, for she had found the blade of a razor, and he had said to her, "Give me that, and I am off"—6 s. 6 d. was afterwards found on Sullivan in my presence—among it was a new half-crown, which was similar to the one I had had in my pocket, and this sixpence now produced, which I can swear to.
WILLIAM STONE ( police-constable V 237.) I searched Edwards—I found nothing on her but a halfpenny, a knife, and fork.
GEORGE GRIFFITHS , ( police-constable M 126.) I apprehended Sullivan in the course of the day, in a public-house—I found 6 s. 6 d. on him, and this half-crown and this sixpence amongst it.
SULLIVAN'S DEFENCE . I was drinking all night, and knew nothing about it; I laid down, and in the morning I was called to go to London; I was at work the day before, and earned half-a-crown, and the day before 3 s.
Edwards's Defence. I went and had tome bread and cheese; I know nothing about this man.
EDWARDS — NOT GUILTY .
SULLIVAN — GUILTY .**— Transported for Ten Years.

1229. KEZIAH EDWARDS was again indicted for stealing 1 shawl, value 4 s.; the goods of Mary Ann Haggerstone; to which the pleaded
GUILTY .— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18450512-1230]
1230. LEONARD GEORGE PROVO was indicted for stealing 1 cask, value 10 s.; and 25 gallons of vinegar, 32., the goods of Frederick Colling-ridge, his master.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ALEXANDER DAVIS . I am in partnership with Mr. Frederick Collingridge; we are vinegar manufacturers, and live at Camberwell. The prisoner was in our service as town traveller since last May—his salary was 3 l. per week—he was to go about collecting orders, and to hand me or my partner a list of them, with their residences—on the morning of the 2nd of April he
handed me this list of customers and their orders—here is in it the name of Thomas East, Goldsmith-row, Hackney-road, half a hogshead of 22 vinegar—we designate the quality of the vinegar by numbers, from 16 to 24—this was well known to the prisoner—the price of this vinegar to East would be about 3 l., and 10 s. the cask—he had no authority to sell it for a less price than that—I handed the list to my partner, that it might be executed.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When an article was delivered out of your warehouse on an order of this kind, did you book the order to the prisoner? A. We book it to the party we deliver it to, not to the prisoner—we did not hold the prisoner responsible for the amount in any way—he did not receive any commission on sales—we took him on commission in May, but in Nov., from some circumstances, we found it right to alter the plan, and to give him 3 l. a-week instead of commission—he received his 3 l. every Saturday night—we began that in the early part of Dec.—we have paid him nothing in commission since that—the commission he had from May to Nov. was fifteen per cent.—I will swear he was not to have ten per cent after that—the arrangement was made by myself and partner—there was an arrangement that if the commission on his sales would have exceeded 156 l. per year he was to have it made up in commission, but his sales were far less than that—there was no time named when we were to ascertain whether his sales had or had not exceeded 156 l.—we did not contemplate any time, considering that he would hardly make it to the amount—if he had, he would have been entitled to a commission of fifteen per cent on the excess of sales—this order was for Thomas East, Goldsmith-row, Hackney-road, and I presume our carman found Mr. East at that direction—I was at the police-court—Mr. Price attended from Messrs. Potter and Collingridge as our solicitor—he called Mr. East, on the part of the prosecution, but he varied much from what he stated to me in the first instance—there have repeatedly been complaints made of orden having been obtained by the prisoner, and the persons afterwards refusing to take the goods—I have blamed the prisoner for it exceedingly—we never told him to the effect that if it happened again he should be at the cost of the articles, nor conveyed that to him—we blamed him for getting fictitious orders when the people would not take them in, and he had not seen the people—we took him last May—we exacted security from him to the amount of 300 l.
Q. Did you receive securities from him to the amount of at least 800 l.? A. Decidedly, from inquiry they are not worth that—I should think 300 l. was the outside of the value of them—I believe they were papers connected with his wife's property, but I have not seen them—they are in the possession of Potter and Collingridge—they made out the deed—I cannot say personally that I have ever seen that deed—my partner has—I have been told he has been obliged to effect a life assurance to the amount of 200 l.—I have not seen it—very likely my partner has it—I know the prisoner pays an insurance, which is to keep us secure against any defalcation of his—we have been applied to by the prisoner or his solicitor for a statement of our account, pro and con. —we have not given it, and I can give you a reason why—after the charge was made against the prisoner, East went at nine o'clock one night to our foreman's wife—I did not see him, but I received a communication from our foreman, who is not here—I have not the least doubt that East offered the money—when we took the prisoner, he had a connexion—he has done a very good trade
for us, and has increased our business considerably—he brought his connexion with him, and may have obtained us sales to the amount of 1000 l. a-year—that is quite the outside—I state that from recollection—we know all his connexions now—we have employed a party in his stead, and at the same salary—we do not mean to give up his connexion now we have got it—six months is the usual credit we give to our customers.
MR. DOANX. Q. Why did you not give an account to the prisoner? A. Because we are daily finding he has received money and not accounted for it—we did not threaten to discharge him with respect to those gales where the orders were not met.
FREDERICK COLLINGRIDGE . I am partner with Mr. Davies. I remember receiving this list, ( looking at it,) and, in consequence, I gave instructions to one of our carmen to take this vinegar to Mr. East's, in Goldsmith's-row—it was sent on the 3rd of April—it came to 3 l. 10 s.—I made an invoice out to East—it went between nine and ten o'clock in the morning—it is four or five miles from our factory to Goldsmith's-row—in consequence of what I heard, I went to Barking on the 14th of April—I there found a half-hogshead, marked "No. 22," in possession of a man named Stevens, who had never had any dealings with us to my knowledge—our name was branded on the cask.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know Mrs. East? A. I do—I am not aware that I said to her that I did not care about the money for the vinegar—to the best of my recollection, I did not say anything about it.
Q. Did you say if it cost 300 l. you did not care, if you could get a conviction against Provo? A. The words I used were," He deserves to go to the Old Bailey for this"—I did not use the word "conviction" to Mrs. East, to the best of my belief—I swear I did not—we took the prisoner on a verbal contract, as far as my recollection serves;—I really cannot say—it is a year ago—we have securities in the hands of our solicitor—I do not know to what amount—I believe they are to the amount of 250 l. or 300 l.—we have not given directions to our solicitor to hand them over to the prisoner, that they might not be lost in case of a conviction—I am not acquainted with law—I am the brother of Mr. Collingridge—I have given directions not to give the securities up, because we have found out other defalcations—we have not obtained another bill—I think the Magistrate remanded the prisoner three times—I believe he was taken on Sunday evening, the 20th of April, and was examined on Monday, the 21st, then remanded, I think for a week; and then, I think, from the Monday till the Tuesday week, and since then I suppose he has been in Newgate—we preferred this charge on the day he was apprehended.
MR. DOANE. Q. Was not the prisoner taken upon the 4th of May? A. Yes, it was, I had forgotten it—we had a verbal contract with the prisoner last December to pay him 8 l. a week.
LOUISA EAST . I am the wife of Charles East—we live in Goldsmith's-row, Hackney-road—my husband keeps a broker's shop. I remember half a hogshead of vinegar coming to ray husband about twelve o'clock in the morning, on the 3rd of April—I was not aware whose cart it came in—I signed the receipt in the book for the cask—this is the receipt I gave—my husband was out at the time—I did not expect a cask of vinegar—I saw the prisoner in the course of that day—he asked me for the invoice. of the vinegar, and I gave it him—a man named Hallett took away the vinegar—I knew Hallett, but I bad said nothing about him to anybody——I
did not refer the prisoner to a carman—nothing was said about one—Hallett is a carman—he came and took away the vinegar about one o'clock—my husband did not see the cask, he did not come home till about ten o'clock at night.
Cross-examined. Q. What is your husband carrying on now? A. The business of a broker—he has been doing so only a few months—before that he kept a shop in the general line where we now live—we dealt in vinegar at that time, and had given the prisoner one or two orders—my husband has been living in that neighbourhood about twelve years, and twelve months in our present house—Mr. Collingridge, the prosecutor, came to me three or four times—he first came to know something about us, but I cannot tell what.
Q. Did he say he did not care about the money for the vinegar; if it cost 300 l. he did not care, all he wanted was to get a conviction against Provo? A. He never made use of the word "conviction"—he said it was not the money he cared for, if it was 300 l. he would rather lose it than not have the case against him—I think they were the words—I never had any charge against myself—I am not a particular friend of the prisoner's—I do not think I saw him above half a dozen of times—I have no family.
COURT. Q. You had had dealings with him before? A. We had two casks, whether they were together I do not know—the prisoner knew my husband.
GEORGE HALLETT . I live in Dove-row, and am a fishmonger. I have a horse and cart, which I generally go with myself—I never knew the prisoner before this—he came to me, and hired my cart for a little job—I cannot say the time exactly—I went myself with it—he told me to go to East's, in Goldsmith's-row, which is four or five yards from my place—it was about one o'clock—he was to give me 1 s. 6 d. for the job—I was to take the cart to Spitalfields church, and meet him there—I saw Mrs. East at her own place—I took the half-hogshead from East's to Spitalfields church—the prisoner was not there, but he came soon afterwards—he said, "Follow me," and he took me to Widegate-alley, and there, by his direction, I rolled out the half-hogshead to Mr. Sandifer's shop—it remained outside the door—the prisoner told me to go back to Mr. East's, where he would meet me and pay me—I went there—he did not come, and I new saw him till he was in custody—I saw Mr. Sandifer at the door of his shop—I did not speak to him.
ROBERT SANDIFER . I am an oilman, and live in Artillery-passage, Widegate-alley. On the 3rd of April the prisoner came to my shop about ten or eleven o'clock in the morning, as near as I can recollect—he said he bad half a hogshead of vinegar to dispose of—I said, "I am not a buyer of vinegar; vinegars are so low, it is useless for me to lay out money for vinegar"—whether he said it was returned on his hands, or he had it to dispose of, I really cannot recollect—I agreed to buy it—he asked me 30 s. for it—I said, "I will give you 25 s., I won't give you a farthing more"—he said, "If I can't get more I must take that"—he brought me the vinegar between one and two, or about two o'clock—I did not see the carman—I tasted the vinegar, to see that I had vinegar—there was 22 marked on the cask—I gave him 25 s.—he gave me this bill and receipt for the money I paid him: "Mr. Sandifer bought of John Emmett, goods 1 l. 5 s., Settled"—he wrote this in my presence—I did not know anything about him—I recollect since I had some slight knowledge of his features—I did not
know where he lived, or where I could find him—I kept the vinegar eight or nine days, and then sold it to a Mr. Stevens for 30 s.—neither I nor Mr. Stevens could recognize the name on the cask, it was so imperfectly branded.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know what the "22" indicates on the cask of vinegar? A. It indicates, if we have it from respectable houses, that it is 22 strong—the 22 is frequently marked on something under 22, that we may not show we are selling 16 vinegar we put 22 on it—I have it from respectable houses—I never buy of cheap houses—I spiled it and showed the vinegar to Mr. Stevens—I had nothing to test the strength of it.
WILLIAM HENRY STEVENS . I am a grocer and cheesemonger, and live at Barking, in Essex. About the 11th of April I bought this half-hogshead of vinegar of Mr. Sandifer for 30 s., in a regular way of business—it was found at my place.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it worth more than 30 s.? A. A regular vinegar-house traveller called on me, and I told him what I gave for this, and I bought a 20 vinegar better than this was—I am not a judge, but according to my judgment, I did not consider it anything like the quality—there was a name on the cask.
THOMAS PRITCHARD ( police-constable P 22.) I was looking out for the prisoner at the latter end of April—he was taken on Sunday, the 4th of May, at his own house—I told him it was on a charge of stealing a cask of vinegar from Collingridge and Davis—he said he could explain the matter, he had received an order from Mr. East, of Goldsmith-row, when it was sent there Mr. East did not want it, he had then bought it of Mr. East, and sold it to Mr. Sandifer.
COURT to LOUISA EAST. Q. What name does your husband go by? A. He is generally known by the name of Thomas.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18450512-1231]
1231. JOHN KING was indicted for unlawfully obtaining 36 yards of linen cloth, value 2 l. 11 s. 8 d., the goods of Richard Breman, by false pretences.
RICHARD BREMAN . I am a linen-draper, and live at Wandsworth. On the 28th of March the prisoner came to me, and said he had been sent by Mrs. Rote the butcher for twenty-eight yards of linen-cloth—I believed he came from her, and let him have twenty-eight yards of linen-cloth, which came to 2 l. 11 s. 8 d.
ANN ROTE . I am a butcher, and live in Larkhall-lane—I did not send the prisoner for any goods to Mr. Brenam, nor did I receive the property—I did not know the prisoner.
GUILTY .— Confined Four Months.
